## Тест gt-1

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2294. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Riverside Community Leisure Centre: Notices for Users

A. The centre is open from 6.30 a.m. to 10 p.m. Monday to Friday, and from 8 a.m. to 6 p.m. at weekends. On public holidays we follow the weekend timetable, except on Christmas Day and New Year's Day, when the building is closed completely. Please note that the last admission to the pool and gym is forty-five minutes before closing, so that staff have time to clean and lock up. The café keeps slightly shorter hours, opening at 8 a.m. every day and serving its final hot drinks at 4 p.m. During the first two weeks of August, the sports hall will be shut while its floor is replaced; badminton and five-a-side bookings for that period will be moved to the hall at Greenway Primary School, a ten-minute walk away. Questions can be emailed to the centre manager, Priya Nandakumar.

B. Our weekly programme includes yoga, Pilates, circuit training, aqua aerobics and a gentle exercise class for people returning to activity after illness or injury. Most classes last fifty minutes, although the Saturday morning circuit session runs for a full hour and a half. Non-members can reserve a place up to seven days in advance online or at reception. If you can no longer attend, please cancel at least two hours before the start; anyone who fails to turn up three times in a month will lose the right to book ahead for the following four weeks. Newcomers to yoga are encouraged to try the Tuesday evening class, which instructor Oliwia Brennan designed specifically for beginners. Mats and light weights are provided, but we ask you to bring your own towel and a bottle of water. Children aged twelve to fifteen may join adult classes only if a parent or guardian takes part alongside them.

C. All swimmers must shower before entering the water. Children under eight must be accompanied in the pool by an adult aged eighteen or over, and one adult may supervise no more than two such children. The pool is divided into lanes during lunchtime and early-morning sessions: choose the slow, medium or fast lane according to your speed, and always swim on the left. Diving is permitted only at the deep end and only when a lifeguard has removed the barrier rope. Inflatable toys are welcome during family sessions on Sunday afternoons but are not allowed at other times. Glass bottles, food and outdoor shoes must not be taken onto the poolside. Our aquatics supervisor, Desmond Achterberg, reminds users that lifeguards' instructions must be followed immediately; anyone who repeatedly ignores them may be asked to leave and could be banned from future sessions.

D. Non-members pay £5.20 for a single swim and £7 for a gym session or a fitness class. Under-sixteens, students and people over sixty-five receive a reduced rate of £3.50 for any of these activities, provided they can show proof of age or student status. Families of up to two adults and three children can buy a family swim ticket for £14. A ten-visit card, priced at £45, offers better value for regular users and can be shared between members of the same household; however, it expires six months after the date of purchase and cannot be refunded. Badminton courts cost £9 per hour, and racquets can be hired from reception for £1.50 each. Prices were last revised in January and are not expected to change again before the end of the year.

E. Membership gives unlimited use of the pool, gym and classes, plus priority booking: members can reserve class places fourteen days ahead instead of seven. A standard monthly membership costs £32, while a concession membership, available to the same groups who qualify for reduced single prices, costs £22. You can join at reception or online, but your first visit must include a short induction with a member of the gym team, covering the safe use of equipment. There is no joining fee for anyone who signs up before the end of March. Memberships run on a rolling basis, and you may cancel at any time by giving one full month's notice in writing. If you are unable to exercise because of illness, injury or pregnancy, you can freeze your membership for up to three months without charge; a doctor's note may be requested.

#### matching_information: The notices have five sections, A–E. Which section contains the following information? Write the correct letter, A–E. You may use any letter more than once.

- **A** A
- **B** B
- **C** C
- **D** D
- **E** E

**1.** a limit on the number of young children one adult can look after

- en: Section C, the swimming rules, says one adult may supervise no more than two children under eight. Section B mentions children too, but only about joining adult classes with a parent.

- uk: У розділі C, де зібрано правила плавання, сказано, що один дорослий може наглядати не більше ніж за двома дітьми віком до восьми років. У розділі B діти теж згадуються, але лише щодо відвідування занять для дорослих разом із батьками.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: C ✓; GPT-5.4: C ✓; Grok: C ✓.

### Секция 2: Your First Weeks on the Shop Floor

A. Welcome to Calloway's. This section of your induction guide explains the routines you will follow every day on the shop floor. Nobody expects you to remember everything at once, which is why, for your first two weeks, you will be paired with a buddy: an experienced colleague who works the same shifts as you and can answer the small, practical questions that come up constantly when you are new. Please remember, though, that your buddy is not your supervisor. Requests for holiday, changes to your hours and any queries about pay should still go to your section leader.

B. Your uniform will be issued on your first day, and keeping it clean and presentable is your responsibility. Each employee receives two replacement shirts a year free of charge; any further replacements are paid for through a deduction from your wages. Your name badge must be worn whenever you are on the shop floor, because customers who wish to praise or complain about the service they received tend to remember a name far better than a face. Jewellery should be kept simple, and anyone working on the fresh food counters must tie long hair back and wear one of the hairnets provided.

C. Every shift begins at the clocking-in terminal beside the staff room. Aim to arrive at least ten minutes before your official start time so that you can change, put your belongings in a locker and attend the briefing that the duty manager holds at the start of each shift. This rarely lasts more than five minutes, but it covers price changes, promotions beginning that day and any deliveries that are running late, so missing it can leave you unable to answer customers' questions. Personal phones must stay in your locker while you are working. If you are expecting an urgent call, let the duty manager know and a message will be passed to you.

D. Much of your time will be spent refilling shelves, and the key rule here is one you will hear repeated throughout the company: first in, first out. Newer stock goes to the back, and items with the earliest date are moved to the front. It can be tempting to put fresh deliveries straight in front because it is quicker, but this leaves older products hidden until they expire, which costs the store money and creates waste that could easily have been avoided. Any item you find that is past its date must be taken off the shelf at once and recorded on your handheld scanner rather than simply thrown away, so that future orders can be adjusted.

E. Slips on spilled liquids are among the most common causes of accidents in supermarkets. If you discover a spill, stay beside it and ask a colleague to bring a yellow warning sign and cleaning equipment; never leave a spill unattended, even briefly, as a customer could walk straight into it. Heavy goods such as multipacks of bottled water should be lifted with your knees bent and your back straight, as demonstrated in your training video. Last year our regional safety adviser, Joaquín Albarrán, examined injury reports from twelve of our stores and found that most back strains occurred not while deliveries were being unloaded but towards the end of shifts, when staff were tired and hurrying to finish. If a load feels too heavy, use a trolley or ask for help.

F. Customers will regularly ask you where to find things. Wherever possible, walk with them to the right aisle instead of pointing; it takes a few seconds longer but is remembered far longer. If you do not know the answer to a question, say so and find someone who does, since a confident guess usually ends up wasting more of the customer's time. Complaints you cannot settle yourself should be passed to your section leader, and you should never argue with a customer, however unreasonable they seem. Staff trained on the tills will also need to follow the company's policy on age-restricted goods, which is covered separately in Section 4 of this guide.

G. Finally, a word from Deepa Raghunathan, who manages our largest store: 'The new starters who settle in fastest are not the ones who already know everything. They are the ones who ask. I would much rather answer the same question three times than discover a week later that someone has been quietly doing a task the wrong way.'

#### note_completion: Complete the notes below. Choose ONE WORD ONLY from the passage for each answer.

**15.** First two weeks: work alongside a ______, an experienced colleague on the same shifts

- Ключ: **buddy**
- Доказательство: «you will be paired with a buddy: an experienced colleague who works the same shifts as you»
- en: Paragraph A says new staff are paired with a buddy for their first two weeks. 'First two weeks' and 'same shifts' restate the passage.
- ru: В абзаце A сказано, что новых сотрудников на первые две недели закрепляют за напарником. Слова "First two weeks" и "same shifts" пересказывают текст другими словами.
- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що нових працівників на перші два тижні закріплюють за напарником. Слова "First two weeks" і "same shifts" переказують текст іншими словами.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: buddy ✓; GPT-5.4: buddy ✓; Grok: buddy ✓.

**16.** Uniform: two free replacement ______ given each year

### Секция 3: Riders Against the Clock

A. Anyone who has stood at a busy junction in central London, New York or Chicago has probably seen one: a rider with a bag slung across the back, weaving between taxis and buses with a speed that looks almost reckless. The bicycle courier seems like a product of the modern, impatient city, yet the job is considerably older than most people imagine. Its history, with its sudden booms and near-disappearances, offers a surprisingly clear picture of how city dwellers have exchanged information, and how often they have changed their minds about the best way to do it.

B. The story begins in the late nineteenth century, when improved bicycle designs made cycling practical for ordinary people. Telegraph companies quickly saw the advantage. A telegram could cross a continent in minutes, but it still had to be carried from the local office to the person whose name was on the envelope, and that final stretch was often the slowest part of the journey. Offices had long employed boys to run these messages on foot; given bicycles, the same boys could reach far more addresses in a single shift. Most were teenagers, and many were paid by the message rather than by the hour, which encouraged them to ride fast. According to urban historian Beatrix Oyelaran, this arrangement 'turned a scattered group of errand boys into the first real delivery network of the industrial city.' She regards it as the model for every courier service that followed.

C. During the twentieth century, the trade gradually lost ground. As telephones spread into offices and homes, fewer messages needed to be written down and carried at all, and motor vans took over most of the heavier deliveries, so in many cities the cycling messenger became a far less familiar sight. The revival came in the 1970s and 1980s, and it was driven by traffic. Financial and legal districts in large cities had become so congested that a car could take longer to cross a few blocks than a person on foot. At the same time, banks, law firms and advertising agencies depended on original paper documents, often ones that needed a signature, arriving the same day. Courier companies responded by hiring riders, who could slip through stationary traffic and deliver a contract across the city in under twenty minutes. Riders usually received a share of the fee for each job, so the busiest could earn a good living.

D. With the revival came a distinctive culture. Riders communicated with a central dispatcher by two-way radio, developed their own slang and often favoured simple, tough machines, including fixed-gear bikes with few parts to break. Informal races between couriers grew into organised events, some of them international. Yusuf Demirel, who rode in central London for eleven years, remembers the work as 'half sport, half survival'. The survival part was real. Most riders were self-employed, so they had no sick pay, and a single collision could leave them without income for weeks. Demirel recalls that experienced couriers would teach newcomers which streets to avoid at certain times of day, a kind of knowledge that, as he puts it, 'no map could give you.'

E. From the late 1980s, the industry faced its most serious threat yet. Fax machines became standard office equipment and email soon followed, allowing documents to travel between offices in seconds, and demand for paper deliveries fell sharply. Many courier firms closed, but others adapted by changing what they carried. Things that cannot be sent down a cable, such as medical samples, computer parts and items needed urgently on film sets, became an increasingly important part of the business. A study by transport researcher Camille Thornquist, who examined the job records of one mid-sized courier firm, found that documents made up roughly four in every five deliveries in 1990 but fewer than one in three by 2005. Her conclusion is that the firms which survived were those that stopped thinking of themselves as paper carriers.

F. Today, the rider with a bag on the back is more likely to be delivering a takeaway meal than a legal contract. Smartphone apps now assign jobs automatically, replacing the human dispatcher, and many riders use electric bikes that reduce the physical strain of long shifts. Some veterans of the earlier era feel that the sense of community has weakened, since app riders rarely meet each other. Food delivery riders also face many of the same dangers as their predecessors, and in several countries there has been heated debate over whether they should count as employees. Yet the bicycle's basic advantages have not changed. Several cities are now encouraging cargo bikes for the last stage of parcel deliveries, hoping to reduce both congestion and air pollution. More than a century after the first telegraph boys pedalled away from their offices, the courier's essential skill remains the same: getting something small across a crowded city faster than anything else can.

#### matching_headings: The reading passage has six paragraphs, A–F. Choose the correct heading for each paragraph from the list of headings below. There are more headings than paragraphs, so you will not use them all.

- **i** A familiar figure with an unexpectedly long history
- **ii** Speeding up the last leg of a telegram's journey
- **iii** Decline, then a comeback powered by gridlock
- **iv** A shared identity built on hard and risky work
- **v** Adapting to survive the arrival of electronic messages
- **vi** New technology, new cargo, but the same core purpose
- **vii** The invention of the modern bicycle
- **viii** How courier races became international competitions
- **ix** Why cities are turning against delivery riders

**28.** Paragraph A

- Ключ: **i (A familiar figure with an unexpectedly long history)**

Усовершенствованные конструкции велосипедов упоминаются лишь мимоходом, поэтому заголовок об изобретении велосипеда описывает деталь, а не главную мысль.

- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: vi ✓; GPT-5.4: vi ✓; Grok: vi ✓.

#### sentence_completion: Complete the sentences below. Choose NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS from the passage for each answer.

**36.** Unlike cars, riders could find a way through ______ and carry a contract across town in less than twenty minutes.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**38.** What point does the writer make in the first paragraph?

- A. Couriers ride more dangerously than other road users.
- B. The courier's job first appeared in London.
- C. Couriers are found mainly in financial districts.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**39.** What does Beatrix Oyelaran say about the early telegraph messengers?

- A. Bicycles turned them into an organised delivery system.
- B. They were the first young workers to be paid by the hour.
- C. They made sending a telegram cheaper.
- D. They were more reliable than adult messengers.

- Ключ: **A (Bicycles turned them into an organised delivery system.)**
- Доказательство: «'turned a scattered group of errand boys into the first real delivery network of the industrial city.'»

- uk: В абзаці B Oyelaran стверджує, що така система перетворила розрізнених хлопчиків на побігеньках на справжню мережу доставки. У тексті сказано, що багатьом посильним платили за кожне повідомлення, а не погодинно, але там немає ні порівняння з дорослими, ні міркувань про вартість.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: A ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**40.** According to Yusuf Demirel, experienced couriers

- A. taught new riders how to read city maps.
- B. shared practical knowledge about streets that maps could not provide.
- C. refused to work in certain parts of the city.

- uk: В абзаці D Demirel розповідає, що досвідчені кур'єри вчили новачків, яких вулиць уникати в певний час, — таких знань не дала б жодна мапа. Вони уникали деяких вулиць у певні години, але не відмовлялися працювати в цілих районах.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: C ✓; GPT-5.4: C ✓; Grok: B ✓.

## Тест gt-2

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2311. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Westbrook College: Evening Courses This Autumn

A. Look After Your Own Car. Tired of paying a garage for jobs you suspect you could manage yourself? This practical eight-week course, led by former mechanic Mateusz Halloran, covers the routine checks every driver should know: tyre pressures and tread, oil and coolant levels, changing a wiper blade and replacing a bulb. In the final two sessions he will show you how to change a wheel safely at the roadside. Classes run on Tuesdays from 7 to 9 p.m. in the workshop behind the main car park, and numbers are limited to ten so that everyone gets hands-on time. Overalls are lent to all students, but you will need to bring your own pair of work gloves and wear sturdy shoes; trainers are not permitted in the workshop. You do not need to bring a car – we use the college's two training vehicles. Fee: £95 (£60 for those over 65).

B. Italian for the Holidays. If you are heading to Italy next year and want to do more than point at menus, this relaxed ten-week course is for you. Tutor Rosalind Kwapong, who lived in Bologna for twelve years, focuses on the language you actually need when travelling: ordering food, asking for directions, buying train tickets and making small talk with your hosts. Grammar is kept to a minimum in her classes, and most of each Thursday evening session is spent practising in pairs. The course is aimed at complete beginners. If you studied some Italian at school and are unsure whether the course is right for you, Rosalind is happy to have a short telephone chat with you before you enrol – just leave your number with the college office. The course book is included in the fee of £110, and audio recordings can be downloaded free from the college website.

C. Real Bread at Home. Learn to bake loaves that put supermarket bread to shame. Over six Wednesday evenings in our newly refurbished teaching kitchen, Noriko Fairweather, who runs her own neighbourhood bakery, will guide you through basic white and wholemeal loaves, flatbreads, bread rolls and, in the final week, a sourdough made from a starter you will take home and keep alive yourself. Everything you bake goes home with you, so bring a large container or cloth bag each week. The course fee of £75 does not cover ingredients; instead, please pay £4 in cash to Noriko at the start of each session. Please note that, although no nuts are used in our recipes, the kitchen is shared with other classes and we cannot guarantee a nut-free environment. Aprons are provided; please tie back long hair.

D. Get More from Your Phone. Do you own a smartphone or tablet but use it for little more than calls? In this patient, unhurried course, Emeka Castellano and his team of volunteers will show you how to make video calls to family, take and share photographs, shop online safely and spot the warning signs of a scam message. The course runs for five weeks. Sessions take place in the college library on Monday evenings from 6 to 7.30 p.m., slightly earlier than most of our classes, so that learners can travel home before it gets too late. Please bring your own device, fully charged, along with any passwords you already use – written down if necessary. The course is free for anyone who lives in the borough; simply show proof of address, such as a recent utility bill, when you enrol. Others pay £30.

E. Watercolour for Beginners. Always wanted to paint but never known where to start? Artist Saoirse Patil will share her favourite techniques, from mixing colours and laying a flat wash to painting skies, trees and water. The course lasts eight weeks and meets on Thursday evenings in Studio 3 on the second floor. You will need paper, brushes and a small set of paints. If you would rather not buy these separately, a starter kit containing everything required can be bought from the college reception for £22. At the end of term, students are invited – though not obliged – to show one piece of work in a small exhibition in the college entrance hall. Fee: £85. Places on this popular course fill quickly, so early enrolment is advised.

### Секция 2: Lifting and Screen Work: A Guide for Staff

A. Most people think of office work as low-risk, and compared with a building site or a warehouse it usually is. Yet the injuries that do occur in offices tend to build up slowly rather than happen in a single moment. Aching shoulders, a stiff lower back and tired eyes rarely send anyone to hospital, but they are among the commonest reasons staff here take time off. This guide explains what you can do yourself and what support the company provides. It was put together by our occupational health nurse, Farida Rahimi, with help from colleagues across the building.

B. Lifting is where office staff are most likely to hurt themselves suddenly. Boxes of printer paper, archive files and returned equipment are heavier than they look, and people often grab them in a hurry. Before you lift anything, test the weight by tipping one corner. If you cannot raise it comfortably, do not attempt to carry it alone. Keep the load close to your waist, bend at the knees rather than the hips, and avoid twisting your body while you hold it; turn by moving your feet instead. Bruno Esposito, our facilities manager, points out that two trolleys are kept beside the post room on every floor, and that staff should book them rather than carry stock up the stairs. He has also asked suppliers to deliver paper in smaller packs, so nobody needs to lift a full carton.

C. If something is too heavy, ask for help or report it. Rafael Takahashi, the staff health and safety representative, says many employees worry that asking for assistance will make them look weak or slow. "The opposite is true," he says. "Someone who stops to get a second pair of hands is doing their job properly." He adds that any strain or near miss should be recorded in the incident book at reception, even if you feel fine afterwards, because a pattern of small reports often reveals a problem with how a task is organised.

D. Screen work causes discomfort in a quieter way. Sitting in one position for hours places constant pressure on the same muscles, and the effects may not appear for weeks. The top of your monitor should be roughly level with your eyes, and the screen about an arm's length away. Your feet should rest flat on the floor or on a footrest, and your forearms should be roughly horizontal when you type. Laptops are a particular concern: because the screen and keyboard are joined, it is impossible to set both at the right height. Bruno Esposito's team will supply a separate keyboard and a stand to anyone using a laptop for more than an hour a day.

E. Changing position matters at least as much as perfect posture. Physiotherapist Akosua Brandt studied sixty staff at a regional call centre over six months. Those who stood up or walked for a few minutes every half hour reported noticeably less neck and back pain than those who took a single long break at lunch, even though both groups spent similar total time away from their desks. Brandt is careful to say that her study was small and that the workers knew they were being observed, so the result should not be treated as final. Even so, she recommends short, frequent breaks over occasional long ones.

F. Your eyes need attention too. Ioana Delacroix, an optometrist who visits the office twice a year, explains that staring at a screen reduces how often we blink, which leaves the eyes dry and sore. She suggests looking at something across the room from time to time to let the focusing muscles relax. She also stresses that screen work does not damage eyesight permanently, but it can reveal an existing problem that a person had not noticed. Anyone who uses a screen regularly is entitled to an eye test paid for by the company, and if you need glasses specifically for screen work, the company will contribute towards their cost.

G. Finally, every new member of staff must complete a workstation assessment within their first fortnight. The form takes about fifteen minutes and is reviewed by Farida Rahimi, who may arrange a desk visit if anything looks wrong. You should repeat the assessment whenever you move desks or your duties change significantly. Rahimi admits that many people treat the form as a box-ticking exercise, but she says the honest answers are what allow her team to fix problems before they turn into injuries.

### Секция 3: What Our Animals Give Us

A. Walk down almost any residential street and you will pass evidence of a quiet devotion: a bag of dog biscuits in a shopping basket, a scratching post glimpsed through a window, a hutch at the bottom of a garden. In many wealthy countries, roughly half of all households now include at least one animal, and owners happily spend sums on food, toys and veterinary care that would have astonished their grandparents. Yet from a purely practical point of view, most modern pets do very little. They do not pull ploughs, guard grain or bring home dinner. So why do so many of us choose to share our homes with them?

B. Part of the answer lies in history. Dogs were the first animals to be domesticated, descending from wolves that began living alongside human groups at least 15,000 years ago. Those early dogs earned their place by helping with hunting and by warning of approaching danger. Cats arrived by a different route. When people began farming and storing grain, the stores attracted mice, and the mice attracted wild cats, which moved in of their own accord and appear to have largely domesticated themselves. For thousands of years, then, the relationship was essentially a working arrangement. Keeping animals mainly for company, rather than for the jobs they could do, became widespread only much later, largely within the last two centuries.

C. That shift makes more sense once you look at what happens inside us when we spend time with an animal. Researchers have found that when dogs and their owners gaze at each other, levels of oxytocin, a hormone linked to the bond between parents and babies, rise in both the human and the dog. In a smaller study of her own, the psychologist Dr Siobhan Kearney asked forty volunteers to complete a stressful arithmetic task and then spend ten minutes either sitting quietly or stroking a friendly cat. Those who had been with the cat reported feeling calmer afterwards, and their heart rates returned to normal more quickly. Kearney is careful not to overstate the result. 'It was one group of people in one laboratory,' she says. 'But it fits what owners have been telling us for years.'

D. Pets also reshape our days in ways we hardly notice. A dog needs walking whether or not its owner feels like going out, and studies have repeatedly found that dog owners are, on average, more physically active than people without dogs. Just as important, perhaps, is who they meet along the way. The sociologist Arjun Mehta spent a year interviewing dog walkers in three city parks and concluded that a dog works as what he calls a 'social passport'. Strangers who would never speak to a lone adult will happily stop to ask a dog's name or age, and many of Mehta's interviewees said they knew far more of their neighbours since getting a dog. Cat owners, he admits, were not part of the research.

E. For people living alone, these effects can matter a great deal. Rhys Abernathy, a retired engineer, adopted a former racing greyhound eighteen months after his wife died. 'I had stopped bothering with mealtimes, stopped bothering with most things,' he recalls. 'Suddenly there was someone who needed feeding at seven every morning.' He insists that the dog did not replace human company; instead, it got him out of the house, where human company could find him again.

F. Children are often given a pet in the hope that it will teach them responsibility and kindness. There is some evidence that looking after an animal helps young people understand that others have needs and feelings, although the research is more mixed than many parents assume. In practice, the daily work of feeding a rabbit and cleaning out its hutch frequently falls to adults once the novelty has worn off. That is worth remembering before anyone promises that a new pet will be entirely the child's responsibility.

G. None of this means that a pet is a cure for every difficulty, and it would be dishonest to pretend otherwise. Animals cost money, restrict holidays and can trigger allergies. Their lives are also far shorter than ours, so owners must expect, sooner or later, to grieve. Animal welfare charities report that some pets are handed back within months because their new owners underestimated the commitment involved. A pet chosen on impulse, to fill a gap that really calls for something else, may end up making both animal and owner unhappy.

H. Still, the appeal endures, and perhaps the simplest explanation is the best one. With other people we are always being judged, at least a little, on our looks, our success or our conversation. A dog waiting at the door or a cat settling on a lap asks for none of that. What pets offer, more than anything, is a kind of acceptance that is rare elsewhere in life, and for many owners that alone is worth every vet's bill.

## Тест gt-3

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2277. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Getting Around Harbridge: Your Guide to Fares and Passes

A. Travelling on buses, trams and the river ferry in Harbridge is simplest with a Tidecard, a plastic smartcard that you top up with credit and touch on a yellow reader when you board. The card itself costs £3, which is refunded if you hand it back at a travel centre in good condition. You can also pay with a contactless bank card or phone, and you will be charged exactly the same fares. Paper tickets bought from the driver are still available on buses, but they cost 50p more per journey and cannot be used to change between services, so most regular travellers find them poor value. Please note that cash is not accepted on trams or ferries at all; tickets for these must be bought from the machines at stops and piers before you travel.

B. The city is divided into three fare zones, with Zone 1 covering the central area around the old harbour. A single journey within one zone costs £1.80, while a trip that crosses into a second or third zone costs £2.60. Once you have touched in, you may switch to any other bus or tram within 60 minutes without paying again; this is known as the hopper period. To protect frequent travellers, the system also sets a daily cap: however many trips you make, you will never pay more than £6.50 in a day for Zones 1 and 2, or £8 if your travel includes Zone 3. The cap applies automatically, as long as you use the same card or device for every journey. Remember to touch out when you leave a tram, because if you forget, you will be charged the maximum fare.

C. If you commute every day, a season pass may save you even more than the daily cap. A weekly pass for all zones costs £29 and runs for seven days from its first use, not from the date of purchase. Monthly passes can start on any date you choose and may be loaded onto your Tidecard online up to two weeks in advance. Annual passes are the cheapest option per journey, and many larger employers in Harbridge offer them through a salary scheme, which spreads the cost across twelve monthly deductions from your pay. Ask your human resources department whether your workplace takes part. Lost or stolen passes can be replaced for a £5 fee, provided the card was registered in your name online beforehand; unregistered cards cannot be protected in this way.

D. Children under five travel free at all times when accompanied by a fare-paying adult. Those aged 5 to 15 pay half the adult fare, and a Youth Tidecard, which requires a passport-style photo, extends this discount until a young person's eighteenth birthday. Full-time students at local colleges and universities can apply for a 30% reduction on season passes, though not on single fares. Residents aged 66 or over, as well as people with certain disabilities, are entitled to a Freedom Pass, which gives free off-peak travel on every service. Off-peak means after 9.30 a.m. on weekdays and all day at weekends and on public holidays. Freedom Pass holders who are unable to travel alone can ask for an additional card allowing a carer to accompany them free of charge.

E. Travel centres at Central Station and Quayside Interchange are open from 7 a.m. to 7 p.m., Monday to Saturday, and staff there can deal with refunds, card registration and lost property. If you believe you have been overcharged, you can claim the difference online within 28 days of the journey. According to Nadia Ferrante, who manages the customer service team, the most common cause of overcharging is 'card clash': when a passenger holds a purse or wallet containing two contactless cards against the reader, the system may take payment from the wrong one, and the daily cap will then not work properly. 'We always advise people to keep their travel card separate,' she says. From next spring, real-time arrival information will also be shown on screens at every tram stop, and the transport authority is inviting residents to suggest which bus stops should be next to receive them.

#### matching_information: The leaflet has five sections, A–E. Which section contains the following information? Write the correct letter, A–E. You may use any letter more than once.

- **A** A
- **B** B
- **C** C
- **D** D
- **E** E

**1.** a way of paying that costs more than the other options

- en: Section A says paper tickets bought from the bus driver cost 50p more per journey, while card and contactless payments are charged the same fares.
- ru: В разделе A сказано, что бумажные билеты, купленные у водителя автобуса, стоят на 50 пенсов дороже за поездку, а при оплате картой и бесконтактным способом тариф одинаковый.
- uk: У розділі A сказано, що паперові квитки, придбані у водія автобуса, коштують на 50 пенсів дорожче за поїздку, а при оплаті карткою та безконтактно тариф однаковий.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: A ✓.

**2.** the length of time allowed for changing to another service without paying again

- Доказательство: «you may switch to any other bus or tram within 60 minutes without paying again»

- ru: В разделе B объясняется, что такое "hopper period": в течение 60 минут можно пересесть на другой автобус или трамвай без доплаты. В разделе A говорится лишь, что по бумажным билетам нельзя делать пересадки, а ограничение по времени не указано.
- uk: У розділі B пояснюється, що таке "hopper period": протягом 60 хвилин можна пересісти на інший автобус чи трамвай без доплати. У розділі A сказано лише, що з паперовими квитками не можна робити пересадки, а обмеження за часом не вказано.

- uk: У розділі D сказано, що студенти отримують знижку 30% на сезонні проїзні, але не на разові квитки, тобто знижка діє лише на один вид квитків.

### Секция 2: Part-Time Vacancies at the Saltmarsh Hotel

A. The Saltmarsh Hotel, a family-run seafront hotel with ninety rooms in the town of Kestrel Bay, is looking for part-time staff to join us before the busy summer season begins. We currently have openings in housekeeping, in our restaurant and on the front desk. Whether you are a student hoping to earn money around your timetable, a parent who needs hours that fit in with the school day, or a retired person who misses being part of a team, we would be delighted to hear from you. For most positions, you do not need to have worked in a hotel before, because full training will be given.

B. Housekeeping assistants prepare guest rooms after departures, changing bedding, cleaning bathrooms and restocking supplies. Shifts usually run from 9 a.m. to 1 p.m., and as Saturdays and Sundays are our busiest changeover days, weekend availability is essential for this role. Restaurant assistants serve breakfast and evening meals, set tables and help keep the dining room tidy; evening shifts can finish as late as 11 p.m., and anyone serving alcohol must be at least eighteen. Front desk staff check guests in and out, take bookings by phone and deal with questions about the local area. You should be confident using a computer, and while the ability to speak a second language will be viewed favourably, it is not a requirement.

C. Pay is above the national minimum wage, and all staff receive a free meal during any shift of five hours or more. Holiday pay is built up in proportion to the hours you work. Once you have completed three months with us, close family members can stay at the hotel at thirty per cent off the usual room rate, subject to availability. Uniforms are supplied free of charge, although you will need to provide your own plain black shoes. We offer between twelve and twenty-five hours a week, and rotas are published two weeks in advance.

D. In return, we ask for reliability and a friendly attitude. If you are unwell and cannot come in, you must phone the duty manager at least two hours before your shift is due to start; messages sent by text or email are not accepted for absence. Personal mobile phones must be left in the staff lockers while you are working. Leilani Chowdhury, who now supervises our restaurant, says: 'I started with two evening shifts a week while I was at college, and the hotel always worked around my exams. Three years later I'm still here. The best part is that every day is different, because you never know who will walk through the door.'

E. To apply, download an application form from the 'Careers' page of our website, or collect a paper copy from reception. Please note that we cannot accept a CV on its own, as the form asks for information that most CVs do not include. Complete every section and attach a covering letter of no more than one page saying which department interests you and which days you are free to work. You can return your application by email to jobs@saltmarshhotel.example or hand it in at the front desk in a sealed envelope addressed to Teodora Wainwright, our Human Resources Manager. The closing date is Friday 21 March.

F. Within ten days of the closing date, shortlisted applicants will be contacted by telephone and invited to an informal interview with the head of the relevant department; housekeeping candidates, for example, will meet Dariusz Okafor, our Head Housekeeper. Those who do well at interview will then be offered a three-hour trial shift, paid at the normal rate. This lets you see whether the work suits you, and lets us see you in action. Finally, we will ask for the names of two referees, one of whom should be a recent employer or teacher. References from relatives cannot be accepted.

G. Successful applicants must attend an induction morning before their first shift. This covers fire safety, food hygiene and our booking system, and your uniform will be fitted on the same day. Please bring your passport or another document proving your right to work in the country, together with your bank details. Teodora's advice to applicants is simple: 'Be honest about when you can work. We would rather hire someone for two reliable shifts a week than someone who promises five and manages three.'

### Секция 3: Back on the Street

A. On a Saturday morning in a mid-sized town, a car park that sits empty for most of the week is transformed. By eight o'clock, rows of striped awnings have gone up, the smell of fresh bread and frying onions drifts between the stalls, and a steady crowd is moving slowly from one table to the next. Scenes like this were becoming rare a generation ago, when many people assumed that the outdoor market was a relic that would quietly disappear. Instead, in towns and cities across many countries, street markets are enjoying a remarkable second life, and they are attracting customers who would once never have thought of shopping outdoors.

B. The decline had several causes. As car ownership spread in the second half of the twentieth century, families began doing one large weekly shop at supermarkets, where everything they needed could be found under one roof and parking was free. Home refrigerators and freezers meant that fresh food no longer had to be bought every day or two, which removed one of the market's great advantages. Out-of-town shopping centres drew trade away from high streets more generally, and as customer numbers fell, many traders retired without anyone to replace them. In some places the market shrank to a handful of stalls selling cheap household goods, which only reinforced the impression that it belonged to the past.

C. So what has changed? Part of the answer lies, perhaps surprisingly, with the internet. When almost anything can be ordered online and delivered the next day, buying in person has become less of a necessity and more of an outing. People increasingly go to a market not simply to collect groceries but to enjoy the atmosphere, try something unfamiliar and talk to the person who grew the vegetables or baked the cake. Growing interest in where food comes from has helped too: many shoppers like being able to ask how a cheese was made or why the tomatoes look different from those in a supermarket. For them, the conversation is part of what they are paying for.

D. Markets have also become attractive to people starting a business. Renting a shop means signing a lease, fitting out the premises and paying bills long before the first customer walks in, whereas a market pitch can often be hired for a single day. Ayumi Kerrigan began selling home-made dumplings from a folding table four years ago. 'I had no idea whether anyone would want them,' she says. 'The stall let me experiment cheaply. If a filling didn't sell, I simply stopped making it the following week.' Within two years she had a loyal following and enough savings to open a small café. She still keeps her stall, though, partly because it is where new customers first discover her food.

E. The benefits may extend beyond the stalls themselves. Viktor Szabo, an urban economist, spent a year counting pedestrians in one town centre and interviewing nearby shopkeepers. He found that on market days, cafés and shops within a few streets of the market reported noticeably more visitors than on other days of the week, and several owners said they now timed special offers to coincide with the market. Szabo is careful not to overstate his findings. 'This was one town with a well-established market,' he points out. 'A new market in a struggling area might not have the same effect, and it could even compete with existing shops.' Still, he believes his results challenge the idea that markets simply take trade away from the high street.

F. Not everyone welcomes the revival, however. In some cities, markets that once sold inexpensive fruit, vegetables and clothing have been redeveloped as 'food halls' or weekend attractions aimed at visitors, with pitch fees to match. Long-standing traders who relied on small profit margins have found themselves unable to afford a place, and some of their regular customers, often older or on low incomes, complain that there is now little they can buy. Residents living close to busy markets have raised other concerns, such as noise, litter and early-morning deliveries. Critics argue that a market which no longer serves its own neighbourhood has lost something important, however popular it may be.

G. Graciela Moutinho, who manages a weekly market in a former railway yard, thinks the answer lies in careful planning. Her market reserves around a third of its pitches for stalls selling everyday essentials, and these traders pay a reduced fee, which is partly covered by higher charges for the more fashionable food stands. She also holds a monthly meeting at which residents can raise problems directly with stallholders. 'A good market should feel like it belongs to the people who live nearby,' she says. 'Visitors are welcome, but if we only cater for them, we will end up as just another tourist attraction.' It is a balance that many organisers are still trying to find, but the crowds filling car parks every weekend suggest it is worth the effort.

#### matching_headings: The passage has seven paragraphs, A–G. Choose the correct heading for each paragraph from the list of headings below. There are more headings than paragraphs, so you will not use them all.

- **i** Why new traders soon move into permanent shops
- **ii** Research suggesting that neighbouring businesses gain too
- **iii** How changing habits emptied the markets
- **iv** Shopping as an outing rather than a chore
- **v** Rules introduced to reduce noise and litter
- **vi** An unexpected return for a fading tradition
- **vii** Fears that markets are leaving local people behind
- **viii** A cheap way to try out a business idea
- **ix** The online threat to face-to-face selling
- **x** One organiser's formula for keeping a market fair

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**35.** According to paragraph B, how did home refrigerators affect street markets?

- A. They encouraged families to shop at out-of-town centres.
- B. They took away the need to buy fresh food so often.
- C. They made it harder for traders to keep food fresh.
- D. They made supermarket food cheaper than market food.

- Ключ: **B (They took away the need to buy fresh food so often.)**
- Доказательство: «fresh food no longer had to be bought every day or two»
- en: Paragraph B says that with fridges and freezers people did not need to buy fresh food every day or two, so markets lost an advantage. Out-of-town centres are mentioned in the next sentence, but not as a result of fridges.
- ru: В абзаце B сказано, что с появлением холодильников и морозильников людям больше не нужно было покупать свежие продукты каждый день или через день, и рынки потеряли своё преимущество. Загородные торговые центры упоминаются в следующем предложении, но не как следствие появления холодильников.
- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що з появою холодильників і морозильників людям більше не треба було купувати свіжі продукти щодня чи через день, тож ринки втратили свою перевагу. Заміські торгові центри згадано в наступному реченні, але не як наслідок появи холодильників.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: B ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**36.** What point does the writer make about the internet?

- A. It has given market traders a new way to reach customers.
- B. It has reduced the number of people who visit markets each week.
- C. It has turned shopping in person into more of a leisure activity.
- D. It has made shoppers less interested in where food comes from.

- Ключ: **C (It has turned shopping in person into more of a leisure activity.)**
- Доказательство: «buying in person has become less of a necessity and more of an outing»
- en: Paragraph C says that because almost anything can be ordered online, going to shop in person has become an outing. The paragraph actually says interest in where food comes from is growing, not falling.
- ru: В абзаце C сказано, что раз почти всё можно заказать онлайн, поход за покупками вживую превратился в своего рода выход в люди. На самом деле в абзаце говорится, что интерес к происхождению еды растёт, а не падает.
- uk: В абзаці C сказано, що оскільки майже все можна замовити онлайн, похід по покупки наживо перетворився на своєрідний вихід у світ. Насправді в абзаці йдеться про те, що інтерес до походження їжі зростає, а не падає.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: C ✓.

- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: C ✓; GPT-5.4: C ✓; Grok: D ✓.

## Тест gt-4

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2265. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Countryside Cottages to Rent This Season

A. Converted from an eighteenth-century hay barn, Willow Barn sleeps six in three bedrooms, one of which is on the ground floor with its own shower room, making it a sensible choice for guests who find stairs difficult. The open-plan kitchen has a range cooker and a dishwasher, and the enclosed garden backs onto a small stream. Well-behaved dogs are welcome, although we ask that no more than two stay at any one time and that they are kept off the furniture. The nearest village shop is a ten-minute walk along a footpath, but it closes at lunchtime on Sundays, so plan your weekend shopping accordingly. Changeover day is Saturday.

B. Owner Ffion Gallagher spent two years restoring The Old Schoolhouse, a Victorian building in Brindle Green, and the original classroom, with its high ceilings and tall arched windows, now serves as a generous living room. There are two double bedrooms upstairs, plus a sofa bed suitable for a child. Because the building is listed, open fires are not permitted, but underfloor heating keeps the house warm even in midwinter. Ffion lives in the next village and is happy to drop off a welcome basket of local bread, cheese and eggs if you let her know your arrival time in advance. Please note that the lane outside is narrow, and larger vehicles may find parking awkward.

C. Set right on the edge of the marshes at Lower Fenwick, Heron's Rest is a single-storey cottage ideal for birdwatchers and walkers. A pair of binoculars and a shelf of field guides are provided, and the living room window looks directly across the reed beds. The cottage sleeps four. There is no mobile signal at all in the area, although a landline is available for emergencies and the broadband connection works reliably in the kitchen. The track to the cottage can flood after heavy rain between November and February, so at that time of year the owner, Hamid Pereira, contacts guests the day before arrival to suggest a route on foot from the church car park. Wellington boots in several sizes are kept in the porch.

D. This is not a cottage for everyone. Hilltop Bothy, on Carran Moor, has no road access: it is reached by a fifteen-minute uphill walk from the nearest parking area, and all supplies must be carried in. Electricity comes from solar panels, which provide enough power for lighting and charging phones but not for hairdryers or electric kettles; cooking is on a gas stove, and a wood-burner heats the single room. In return, guests enjoy what may be the finest views in the county and some of the darkest night skies. The bothy sleeps two, and bookings are for a minimum of three nights. Children under twelve cannot be accommodated because of the steep drop close to the door.

E. Families return to Orchard Cottage, in Stoke Abley, year after year. The large garden includes a climbing frame, a trampoline surrounded by safety netting, and an orchard where guests are free to pick apples and plums in season. Inside, there are four bedrooms, a games room with a table-tennis table, and a cupboard stocked with board games. A travel cot and a high chair can be supplied on request at no extra cost. The cottage is two miles from the nearest town, which has a supermarket, a swimming pool and a weekly farmers' market. Pets are not allowed, as the owners' hens roam the garden freely.

F. Perched on the cliffs above a sandy cove at Penmarrow Head, The Lookout was originally built for a coastguard and has been modernised by its current owner, Ruth Calloway, who added a glass-walled sitting room on the upper floor. Steps lead down from the garden gate to the beach, although they are steep and unsuitable for pushchairs. The cottage sleeps five and has a washing machine and an outdoor shower for rinsing off sand. Outside the school summer holidays, short breaks of two nights are accepted; in July and August, it is let by the week only. The coastal path passes the front door, and a café at the top of the cove serves breakfast from eight o'clock.

#### matching_information: The text has six descriptions of holiday cottages, labelled A–F. Which description contains the following information? Write the correct letter, A–F. You may use any letter more than once.

- **A** A
- **B** B
- **C** C
- **D** D
- **E** E
- **F** F

- uk: В абзаці D сказано, що діти до дванадцяти років не можуть зупинятися в "bothy". В абзаці F згадано, що сходинки незручні для дитячих візочків, але це лише порада, а не правило щодо віку гостей.

### Секция 2: Flexible Working Policy: A Guide for Staff

A. This policy explains how employees of Corran Freight Services can vary their working hours or work from home. It applies to all permanent staff who have completed their probationary period, which normally lasts six months. Temporary and agency workers are not covered, although managers may agree informal arrangements with them on a case-by-case basis. The policy was written by Zainab Holloway, our Head of People, after a staff survey in which more than half of respondents said that greater control over their schedule would improve their working lives.

B. Under our flexitime scheme, you may start work at any point between 7 a.m. and 10 a.m. and finish between 3.30 p.m. and 7 p.m., as long as you complete your contracted hours over each four-week period. Everyone must, however, be available during the 'core hours' of 10 a.m. to 3 p.m., when most meetings are scheduled and customers are most likely to call. Lunch breaks must last at least 30 minutes and should be taken between noon and 2 p.m. Hours worked beyond your contract can be carried forward, up to a maximum of twelve, and exchanged for time off; any extra above that limit will be lost unless your manager has approved it in advance as overtime.

C. Employees whose roles can be done remotely may work from home for up to two days a week. Staff in the warehouse, at reception and in the vehicle workshop are excluded, because their duties require them to be on site. On home-working days you are expected to be contactable by phone and through the company messaging system throughout core hours, and to attend video meetings with your camera switched on unless there is a good reason not to. Home-working days should be fixed in advance and recorded in the shared team calendar so that colleagues know where everyone will be. Teams are asked to agree one 'anchor day' each week when all members come into the office.

D. To request a flexible arrangement, complete the online application form on the staff portal and submit it to your line manager. You should explain which pattern you would like, when you would like it to begin and how you think any effect on your team could be managed. Your manager will arrange a meeting with you within fourteen days and will give you a written decision no later than one month after receiving the form. If the request is turned down, the reasons will be explained in writing, and you may appeal to the People Team within ten working days. You may make one formal request in any twelve-month period, although minor changes, such as swapping which day you spend at home, can be agreed informally.

E. Before you begin working from home, you must complete a short self-assessment of your workspace, which covers lighting, seating and the position of your screen. The company will provide a laptop, a headset and, if needed, a separate monitor; these remain company property and must be returned if you leave. We do not pay for home internet or heating, but a monthly allowance of £20 is added to the salary of anyone who works from home regularly. Technical problems should be reported to the IT helpdesk, run by Piotr Nakamura; he asks staff to include a photograph of any error message they see. Confidential paper documents must never be taken home; if you need to print sensitive material, do so in the office.

F. All arrangements begin with a trial period of three months, after which you and your manager will discuss whether the pattern is working and record the outcome on your file. An arrangement may be ended or changed if it is causing clear difficulties for the business, for example if customer response times become noticeably slower, but you will normally be given four weeks' notice. Likewise, you may return to your previous pattern at any time by informing your manager in writing.

G. This policy will itself be reviewed every two years. Questions about it should be directed to the People Team rather than to individual managers, so that the same answer is given to everyone. Ms Holloway has stressed that flexibility is a shared responsibility: it works best when employees plan ahead, keep their colleagues informed and remember that the needs of customers come first.

### Секция 3: The Changing Shape of Sleep

A. Anyone who has cared for a newborn knows that babies do not sleep like the rest of us. A healthy infant may sleep for fourteen to seventeen hours a day, yet rarely for more than three or four hours at a stretch, and with little regard for whether it is day or night. The reason is that the internal body clock, which tells adults when to feel alert and when to feel drowsy, is not yet working properly at birth. It takes several months to develop, helped along by regular feeding, daylight and the routines of the household. Babies also spend about half of their sleep in a lively, dream-rich stage known as REM sleep, in which the eyes dart about beneath closed lids. Researchers believe this active sleep plays an important part in the rapid growth of the young brain. By around six months, many infants manage a longer stretch at night, much to the relief of exhausted parents.

B. As children grow, their sleep gradually gathers itself into one long block at night. Naps become shorter and less frequent, and most children give them up altogether somewhere between the ages of three and five. What remains is some of the deepest sleep a person will ever have. Young children can often be carried from a car to their bed without stirring, because so much of their night is spent in slow-wave sleep, the most restful stage. This is also when the body releases much of its growth hormone. Dr Ezinne Lindahl, a paediatric researcher, tracked the sleep of 200 primary-school pupils over a year and found that those who slept least were noticeably more irritable in class, even when their teachers had not been told who was short of sleep. In her view, a regular bedtime is one of the simplest gifts a parent can give.

C. Then comes adolescence, and with it a change that has given teenagers an unfair reputation for laziness. Around puberty, the body clock shifts later, so that the hormone melatonin, which signals that it is time to sleep, is released later in the evening. A fifteen-year-old who cannot fall asleep before eleven o'clock is not simply being stubborn; their biology is pushing bedtime back. Yet teenagers still need around eight to ten hours of sleep, and early school starts leave many of them running short. Some schools have experimented with later timetables. Professor Kofi Haraldsen studied one secondary school that began lessons fifty minutes later. He reported that pupils gained, on average, half an hour of extra sleep each night, and that fewer of them arrived late for the first class.

D. Adulthood brings a more settled pattern, but not necessarily more rest. Most adults need between seven and nine hours a night, yet surveys in many countries suggest that a large share regularly get less. Long working hours, commuting, screens and young children all compete for the time once given to sleep. Many people also live with what scientists call 'social jet lag': they go to bed and wake up at very different times on working days and at weekends, which unsettles the body clock in much the same way as flying across time zones. Sleep researcher Valentina Brooksby compared office workers on regular and irregular weekly schedules. She found that the irregular group reported feeling tired more often, even when their total hours of sleep were similar.

E. In later life, sleep changes again. Older adults typically spend much less time in deep sleep and wake more often during the night, sometimes simply because a noise or a full bladder disturbs a lighter sleep. The body clock also tends to move earlier, the reverse of the teenage shift, so many people find themselves drowsy by nine in the evening and wide awake before dawn. Daytime naps, abandoned in early childhood, often return. It is widely assumed that older people need less sleep, but most experts disagree: the need stays much the same; it is the ability to obtain it that declines. Dr Mateo Kinsella interviewed retired people about their nights. He found that many had come to accept poor sleep as an unavoidable part of ageing and had never mentioned it to a doctor.

F. What, then, can be learned from this lifelong journey? Firstly, there is no single 'correct' way to sleep: a pattern that is perfectly normal at one age may be a sign of trouble at another. Secondly, light is a powerful ally at every stage. Morning daylight helps babies develop a rhythm and helps teenagers feel sleepy at a more reasonable hour, while light in the late afternoon can help older adults who find themselves nodding off too early. Finally, regularity matters more than many people realise. Going to bed and getting up at roughly the same times, weekends included, supports the body clock whatever one's age. Sleep may change its shape over the decades, but its importance never fades.

- **viii** A clock that stops working in old age
- **ix** The financial cost of tired employees

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**38.** What do researchers believe about the active sleep of babies?

- A. It lets babies sleep longer at night.
- B. It helps the young brain develop quickly.
- C. It makes regular feeding easier to set up.
- D. It keeps the eyes rested beneath the lids.

- Ключ: **B (It helps the young brain develop quickly.)**
- Доказательство: «Researchers believe this active sleep plays an important part in the rapid growth of the young brain»
- en: Paragraph A links REM, or active, sleep to the rapid growth of the young brain. The eyes are said to move during this sleep, not to rest, and longer night sleep comes later with age, not from REM sleep.
- ru: Абзац A связывает фазу REM, или активный сон, с быстрым ростом мозга у младенцев. Сказано, что во время этого сна глаза двигаются, а не отдыхают, а более длинный ночной сон появляется позже, с возрастом, а не благодаря фазе REM.
- uk: Абзац A пов'язує фазу REM, або активний сон, зі швидким ростом мозку немовлят. Сказано, що під час цього сну очі рухаються, а не відпочивають, а довший нічний сон з'являється пізніше, з віком, а не завдяки фазі REM.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: B ✓.

## Тест gt-5

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2257. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Larchfield Court: Notices for Residents

A. From 1 March, the council will collect recycling from Larchfield Court every Tuesday instead of every other Friday. The bins in the rear courtyard have also been replaced. Blue bins now take paper, cardboard, cans and plastic bottles together, so there is no longer any need to sort these items. Glass, however, must still go in the separate green bank next to the bicycle shed. Please flatten all boxes before putting them in a bin; a single unflattened box can fill half the space, and last month two bins were left uncollected because they were overflowing. Food waste caddies are available free of charge from the caretaker, Declan Abiodun, whose office is beside the lift on the ground floor. Larger items such as furniture or old appliances must never be left in the courtyard. The council will remove up to three bulky items for a small fee if you book a collection online.

B. All repairs inside your flat should now be reported through the residents' portal rather than by phone, so that each request receives a reference number and can be tracked. If you do not have internet access, a paper form can be picked up from the caretaker's office. Routine jobs, such as a dripping tap or a sticking window, will normally be dealt with within ten working days. Emergencies are a different matter: a burst pipe, a total loss of heating in winter or a door that cannot be locked should be reported immediately on the 24-hour emergency line, and an engineer will aim to attend within four hours. Please do not attempt to repair gas appliances yourself under any circumstances. Problems in shared areas, including broken lights on the stairs, can be reported to Declan directly, as he handles minor repairs himself.

C. Following complaints from residents who regularly found their spaces occupied, the residents' committee has agreed to introduce a permit scheme for the underground car park. From 15 April, every vehicle parked there must display a valid permit on the windscreen. Each flat is entitled to one permit, which will be issued free of charge; a second permit can be requested, but these will only be granted if spaces remain once all first permits have been allocated. Visitors may use the four marked bays near the entrance for a maximum of three hours. Vehicles without a permit will receive a warning notice the first time and may be towed away if the offence is repeated. Applications should be sent to the committee secretary, Hiroshi Quarshie, by the end of March.

D. Since the building's new parcel room opened in January, couriers have been leaving packages there when nobody answers the door. You will receive a text message with a four-digit code, which opens the room's electronic lock. Parcels should be collected within five days; after that, they will be moved to the caretaker's office, where they can be picked up during office hours only. Please remember that staff cannot sign for deliveries on your behalf. The room is too small for large deliveries, so if you are expecting something bulky, such as a mattress or a washing machine, please arrange for it to be delivered on a weekday morning, when the service lift can be reserved. To book the lift, contact our concierge, Agnieszka Ruffo, at least 48 hours in advance, as she needs to confirm times with the courier.

E. Most residents are considerate neighbours, but the building manager, Marisol Tennant, has received a growing number of complaints about noise late at night. As a reminder, quiet hours run from 11 p.m. to 7 a.m. on weekdays and from midnight to 9 a.m. at weekends. Building work, including drilling, is only permitted between 9 a.m. and 5 p.m., Monday to Saturday. If you are planning a party, it is good practice to let your neighbours know beforehand. Because the floors in the older part of the building are thin, residents there are asked to place rugs over at least part of any wooden floor. If a noise problem continues, please talk to your neighbour politely first; if this does not help, write to Marisol, and she may arrange a meeting between both households.

#### true_false_not_given: Do the following statements agree with the information given in the notices? Write TRUE if the statement agrees with the information, FALSE if the statement contradicts the information, or NOT GIVEN if there is no information on this.

**1.** Recycling will be collected more frequently than it was before 1 March.

- Ключ: **TRUE**
- Доказательство: «every Tuesday instead of every other Friday»
- en: Notice A says collections change from every other Friday to every Tuesday. Once a week is more often than once every two weeks, so the statement is true.

- uk: В оголошенні A сказано, що сміття вивозитимуть не по п'ятницях раз на два тижні, а щовівторка. Раз на тиждень — це частіше, ніж раз на два тижні, тож твердження правильне (TRUE).
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: T ✓; GPT-5.4: T ✓; Grok: T ✓.

### Секция 2: Handling Complaints: A Guide for Front-Line Staff

A. Every complaint, however unpleasant it may feel at the time, is a chance to put something right. Customers who bother to complain are usually telling us that they want to keep doing business with us; those who are truly finished with a company tend to leave without a word. This section of the booklet explains what you need to know before a complaint reaches you and what you should do once it has. It applies whether the customer speaks to you face to face, phones the helpline or writes to us by email.

B. Your first task is simply to listen. Many staff feel pressure to offer a solution within seconds, but interrupting a customer who is still explaining the problem almost always makes matters worse. Let the person finish, even if you think you already know what they are going to say. Do not be tempted to defend the company at this stage. Take brief notes as they talk, and when they have finished, repeat the main points back to them in your own words. This shows that you have understood, and it gives the customer a chance to correct any misunderstanding before you act on it.

C. Next, acknowledge how the customer feels. This is not the same as admitting that the company is at fault. A sentence such as "I can see why that would be frustrating" recognises the customer's experience without accepting blame for something that has not yet been investigated. Avoid phrases like "calm down" or "that's our policy", which tend to sound dismissive even when they are not meant to. If you do discover that we have made a mistake, however, say so plainly and apologise; customers are far more forgiving of an honest error than of an excuse.

D. Before you promise anything, check what you are allowed to offer. All staff may replace faulty goods or give a full refund on items bought within the last thirty days, provided the customer can show a receipt or a bank statement. Anything beyond this, such as compensation for travel costs or a goodwill voucher, must be approved by a supervisor. Never promise an outcome you are not certain you can deliver: a broken promise turns one complaint into two. If you need to pass the matter on, tell the customer who will deal with it and when they can expect to hear back.

E. Some complaints cannot be settled during the first conversation. In these cases, record the details on the complaints log the same day, using the customer's own description of the problem rather than a summary of your own. Our policy is to send a written acknowledgement within two working days and a full response within ten. Ilse Mbatha, who manages our customer service team, reviewed six months of records last year and found that customers who received an update before the deadline, even one saying only that the investigation was still going on, were much less likely to escalate their complaint. Her review covered only our own branches, but its message is clear: silence is usually read as indifference.

F. Occasionally a customer becomes aggressive. You are not expected to put up with abuse. Stay calm, keep your voice low and steady, and explain politely that you want to help but cannot continue if the shouting goes on. If the behaviour continues, you may end a phone call after one clear warning, or ask a supervisor to take over a face-to-face conversation. Afterwards, write a short account of what happened while it is fresh in your mind. Callixte Duarte, our senior trainer, recommends taking a few minutes away from your desk after any difficult exchange, since carrying tension into the next conversation can affect how you treat a customer who has done nothing wrong.

G. Finally, remember that each complaint contains information. A single complaint about a late delivery may be bad luck; ten in a week suggest a problem with a supplier or a route. When you close a complaint on the log, choose the category that best describes the cause, not merely the symptom. Team leaders read these categories every month, and several of the improvements introduced over the past year, including clearer labels on our returns envelopes, began as patterns that front-line staff had noticed and recorded. The few seconds it takes to choose carefully are well spent.

### Секция 3: Life Behind the Lamp

A. Ask most people to picture a lighthouse keeper and they will probably imagine a solitary figure with a beard and a pipe, gazing out at a stormy sea. It is an attractive picture, but it tells only a small part of the story. For generations, until machines took over their duties, keepers did demanding and often repetitive work on which the lives of sailors depended. Their days were shaped less by dramatic rescues than by routine, and the qualities their employers valued most were reliability and patience rather than courage.

B. The night was the heart of the job. Before electricity reached the coast, the light came from oil lamps whose wicks had to be trimmed regularly so that they burned brightly without smoking; American keepers were nicknamed 'wickies' for this reason. In many towers the great lens turned on a clockwork mechanism driven by a heavy weight, which slowly sank down the centre of the tower and had to be wound back up every few hours. If the keeper on watch fell asleep, the light could stop rotating, and a ship relying on its particular pattern of flashes might mistake one headland for another. For this reason, falling asleep on duty was treated as one of the most serious offences a keeper could commit.

C. Daylight brought a different set of tasks. Oil lamps left a thin film of soot on the lens and windows, and every surface had to be cleaned each morning, while brass fittings were polished and walls repainted to protect them from salt air. Keepers also kept detailed logbooks, recording the state of the light, passing vessels and, above all, the weather. The historian Eilidh Macrae, who examined logbooks from eleven Scottish stations, found that the entries suggested keepers spent more of their waking hours on cleaning and maintenance than on watching the light itself. She is careful to point out, however, that her sample came from a single region and may not reflect conditions elsewhere.

D. Where a keeper lived made an enormous difference to his life. At shore stations, built on headlands, keepers often lived with their wives and children in cottages beside the tower, and wives frequently shared the work, sometimes officially taking over when a husband died. Rock stations, built on isolated reefs or tiny islands, were another matter. Here only men were posted, usually in teams of three, so that if one fell ill or was injured the other two could continue to run the light. They typically worked several weeks at a time before a boat brought their replacements, although a long spell of bad weather could keep the relief boat away for days or even weeks beyond the planned date.

E. Living in such close quarters demanded tact. Gwilym Pritchard, who spent fourteen years on rock stations off the Welsh coast before retiring, recalls that small habits could become intensely irritating. 'You learned not to whistle the same tune twice,' he says. Cooking was shared on a rota, and a man who could not make a decent meal was not popular for long. To fill the quiet hours, keepers took up an impressive range of pastimes. Pritchard taught himself to repair clocks, while one of his colleagues built detailed model ships inside glass bottles. Some keepers grew vegetables in sheltered corners of the rock, although salt spray destroyed much of what they planted. Reading was almost universal, and books were exchanged so often that, as Pritchard puts it, 'every station had the same library by the end of the year.'

F. Change came gradually and then very quickly. Electric lamps removed the need to tend flames, and from the middle of the twentieth century automatic equipment could switch lights on at dusk, change failed bulbs and report faults to a distant control room. One by one, stations lost their keepers, and in Britain the last of them left at the end of the 1990s. Some keepers stayed on as part-time attendants, visiting a light occasionally to check the equipment. Pritchard admits that he felt a mixture of relief and sadness when his last station was automated. 'The machines were more dependable than we were,' he says, 'but a machine never noticed a small boat in trouble.'

G. The keepers' legacy is more than romantic. Many former stations now welcome visitors, and some cottages have been turned into holiday homes. Less obviously, the logbooks themselves have become valuable. Because keepers recorded wind, rain and sea conditions several times a day, often for decades at the same spot, their notes offer scientists a long and consistent record of the coastal climate. Researchers studying how coastal storms have changed over time increasingly turn to these handwritten pages, some of which describe places where no official weather station was ever built. In that sense, the keepers are still keeping watch.
