## Тест gt-6

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2294. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Your Visit to the National Museum

A. Entry to the museum's permanent galleries is free for everyone, although we welcome donations at the boxes beside the main entrance. Our special exhibitions, which change three times a year, do require a ticket. These can be bought online up to a month in advance, and booking ahead is strongly recommended: each ticket is for a timed entry slot, and weekend slots frequently sell out by Friday afternoon. A limited number of tickets are kept back for sale at the front desk each morning. Reduced prices apply to students, people over 65 and those receiving unemployment benefits, while under-fives go free. If you expect to come more than twice in a year, an annual pass usually works out cheaper and lets you skip the queue. The museum is open from 10 a.m. to 5.30 p.m. every day except 24–26 December, with a late opening until 9 p.m. on Fridays.

B. Our volunteer guides lead a free highlights tour twice a day, at 11 a.m. and 2 p.m., starting from the large whale skeleton in the entrance hall. The tour lasts about 45 minutes and covers six of the museum's best-known objects, making it ideal for first-time visitors. There is no need to book, but places are limited to twenty people per tour and are given out on a first-come, first-served basis. Groups of ten or more who would like their own private tour should contact us at least three weeks before their visit. 'People often tell us the tour changed the way they saw the rest of the building,' says Bongani Ashworth, who has guided visitors here for eleven years. If you prefer to explore at your own pace, audio guides in nine languages can be hired from the information desk; a small deposit is charged and returned when the device is handed back. A tour in British Sign Language runs on the first Saturday of every month.

C. Large bags, suitcases and umbrellas are not permitted in the galleries and must be left in the cloakroom on the lower ground floor, where lockers take a one-pound coin that is returned when you collect your belongings. All floors can be reached by lift, and wheelchairs can be borrowed free of charge from the information desk, though they cannot be reserved in advance. Baby-changing facilities are available on every floor. The ground-floor café serves hot and cold food throughout the day, while the rooftop restaurant takes bookings only. Visitors who have brought their own lunch are welcome to use the picnic room next to the cloakroom. For anyone who finds the galleries overwhelming, a quiet room with soft lighting is located on the second floor and is open to all visitors.

D. Children are at the heart of what we do. Families can collect an explorer backpack, containing a magnifying glass, sketching materials and a trail leading to ten hidden animals around the building, from the Family Desk. Backpacks are free, but we ask for a form of photo identification, which we keep until the backpack is returned. 'We designed the trails so that adults learn as much as the children,' explains Imogen Szczepanski, head of learning. 'Parents tell us they notice things they'd walked past for years.' Every Saturday and Sunday, drop-in craft workshops run in the Discovery Studio between 11 a.m. and 3 p.m.; no booking is needed. Our monthly sleepovers allow children aged seven to eleven to spend the night among the dinosaurs. At least one adult must stay with every group of up to five children, and these events usually fill up within days of being announced.

E. The museum is a five-minute walk from Riverside underground station. On-site parking is very limited and reserved for visitors with disabilities, so we encourage everyone else to come by public transport or bicycle; racks can be found by the east gate. Photography for personal use is allowed in the permanent galleries, provided you switch off your flash, but cameras are not allowed in special exhibitions because many objects are on loan from other collections. The museum shop, beside the main exit, stocks books, toys and gifts, and every purchase helps fund our work. Comments can be left at the information desk or on our website.

### Секция 2: Welcome to the Shop Floor: A Guide for New Volunteers

A. Thank you for choosing to give your time to the Northfield Hospice shop. Every item we sell, from a paperback to a winter coat, helps pay for the care of local families, and none of that would be possible without people like you. This part of the handbook explains how the shop runs from day to day and who to turn to when you are unsure. Please read it before your first shift and keep it somewhere handy: nobody expects you to remember everything at once, and asking questions is always better than guessing.

B. Your first point of contact will usually be Ravi Lindeman, our volunteer coordinator. Ravi draws up the weekly rota, so if you cannot make a shift, please let him know at least forty-eight hours beforehand whenever possible. He also runs a short induction for every newcomer, during which you will be shown the fire exits, the first-aid box and the staff kitchen. For their first three shifts, Ravi pairs new volunteers with an experienced 'buddy', and in his experience people who start this way are far more likely to stay with us beyond their first month.

C. Overall responsibility for the shop lies with our manager, Josephine Adeyemi. She sets the weekly sales targets, decides how the front window is dressed and has the final word on any complaint from a customer. Josephine is keen that volunteers should never feel they must handle difficult situations alone. If a customer becomes aggressive, or asks for a refund without a receipt, simply call her, or whoever is in charge that day if she is not in. She also asks that personal mobile phones are kept in the lockers rather than on the shop floor, where they can easily go missing.

D. Most donations arrive in bags at the back door, and these go straight to the sorting room, which is run by Aiko Fitzgerald. Aiko's firm rule is that no bag is opened until it has been felt carefully from the outside, because donors sometimes include broken glass and, very occasionally, used needles. Always wear the gloves provided. Items are then divided into three groups: those fit to sell, those that can go to our textile recycler, and those that must be thrown away. Electrical goods cannot be sold until they have passed a safety test, so they are labelled and placed on a separate shelf to await the engineer's monthly visit. Toys must carry a safety mark; if you cannot find one, the toy goes in the recycling bin rather than on the shelves.

E. Pricing is also Aiko's responsibility, although after a few weeks she may trust you to price clothing yourself. As a general guide, we aim to charge about a third of what the item would cost new, adjusting for its condition and brand. Each price label is coloured according to the week in which the item went on display, which lets anyone see at a glance how long it has been waiting for a buyer. Anything still unsold after four weeks is reduced to half price, and two weeks after that it is passed on to another branch, where it reaches a fresh set of customers.

F. Working on the till is overseen by our deputy manager, Stefan Okwuosa, who trains each volunteer individually before they are allowed to take payments. He will also explain Gift Aid, a government scheme that allows the charity to claim an extra 25 pence for every pound raised from goods donated by taxpayers who have signed a declaration. Because this adds up to a significant sum over a year, please ask every donor whether they would like to sign up. Cash is counted by two people together at the end of each day and is never left in the till overnight.

G. Finally, our regional health and safety adviser, Carmen Haugland, visits every three months. Carmen checks that walkways and fire exits are clear and that heavy stock is stored at waist height rather than up high. She reminds volunteers that no one should lift more than they can comfortably carry; furniture donations, in particular, must always be moved by two people using the trolley kept by the back door. In a recent review of accident reports from the region's shops, she found that most minor injuries happened not during lifting but when volunteers stood on chairs to reach high shelves, so please always use the step stool provided.

### Секция 3: The Quiet Power of Walking

A. For almost all of human history, getting from one place to another meant putting one foot in front of the other. Walking was not a hobby or a fitness plan; it was simply how life worked. Today, in many wealthy countries, even journeys of less than two kilometres are routinely made by car, and the humble act of walking has come to seem slightly old-fashioned. Yet a growing number of doctors, planners and ordinary citizens are rediscovering what our ancestors took for granted: that travelling on foot may be one of the cheapest and most effective ways to improve both our own lives and the places we live in.

B. The physical benefits are well established. Researchers have found that regular brisk walking reduces the risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes and several other serious conditions, and it helps people keep a healthy weight as they age. Health authorities commonly advise adults to do at least 150 minutes of moderate activity each week, and a brisk half-hour walk on five days meets that target. What makes walking so attractive is how little it demands. There is no membership fee, no special kit beyond a decent pair of shoes, and no need to book a court or join a team. It is also gentle on the joints, so people who have never thought of themselves as sporty can start without much fear of injury.

C. The rewards are not only physical. Many people say a walk helps them think more clearly, and there is evidence to support the feeling. In one study, the psychologist Wanjiru Delgado asked 120 office employees to take a twenty-minute walk during their lunch break for six weeks. Half walked through a nearby park, while the others followed a route along a busy main road. Both groups reported feeling less tired in the afternoons than before, but the improvement was noticeably greater among those who had walked among trees and grass. Delgado is careful not to overstate her results: the group was small, and the workers described their own moods rather than being measured by any test. Still, she believes the findings are worth taking seriously.

D. Walking also changes how people relate to one another. A driver passes through a neighbourhood behind glass; a pedestrian is part of it. People on foot nod to neighbours, stop to chat outside the bakery and notice when an elderly resident has not been seen for a few days. Urban thinkers have long argued that streets full of walkers feel safer, because so many people are quietly keeping an eye on what is going on. In areas where nearly every trip is made by car, by contrast, residents may live side by side for years without ever learning each other's names.

E. There is an economic side to the story too. When councils propose removing traffic from shopping streets, local traders often protest, convinced that customers who cannot park outside will simply go elsewhere. The transport planner Matthias Okeke decided to test this fear. He surveyed shoppers on a high street in a medium-sized town before and after it was closed to cars. He found that people arriving on foot tended to spend less on each visit than drivers did, but they came back far more often, so that over a month they spent more in total. Okeke admits that one town cannot speak for every high street, but he considers the pattern hard to ignore. Several shops that had opposed the scheme later asked for it to be extended to neighbouring streets.

F. If walking brings so many benefits, why do so many people avoid it? The usual explanation is laziness, but that is unfair. In much of the modern world, the environment itself discourages walking. Suburbs are often spread out, so that the nearest shop may be a long way from home. Pavements suddenly come to an end, wide roads are difficult and frightening to cross, and pedestrians may wait two minutes at a crossing for a green light that lasts only a few seconds. Even keen walkers give up when a ten-minute stroll means crossing a car park and a six-lane road. Faced with such conditions, choosing the car is a perfectly rational decision.

G. The encouraging news is that walking does not require expensive projects to flourish. Some of the most effective improvements are surprisingly modest: shortening the distance across busy junctions, giving pedestrians more time at traffic lights, planting trees that offer shade in summer, adding benches so that older people can rest, and improving street lighting so that evening walks feel safe. None of these measures costs as much as a new road, yet together they can transform how a place feels. Cities need not wait for grand plans, and nor do individuals. The next time a journey is short enough, leave the keys at home and see what you notice along the way.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

- A. It produces faster results than joining a sports team.
- B. It suits people who already think of themselves as sporty.
- C. It allows adults to meet their weekly target in one day.
- D. It asks for very little in the way of cost or preparation.

- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що для ходьби не потрібні ні членський внесок, ні спеціальне спорядження, ні попередній запис. Тижневу норму виконують, гуляючи п'ять днів, а не один, а за словами автора, ходьба підходить тим, хто НЕ вважає себе спортивною людиною.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: D ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**36.** What did Wanjiru Delgado's study suggest?

- A. Walks among greenery eased tiredness more than roadside walks.

- C. Lunchtime walks helped only the workers who went to the park.
- D. Tests confirmed that walkers were calmer than other workers.

- Ключ: **A (Walks among greenery eased tiredness more than roadside walks.)**
- Доказательство: «the improvement was noticeably greater among those who had walked among trees and grass»
- en: In Paragraph C, both groups felt less tired, but the park group improved more. So it is wrong that only the park group benefited, and no tests were used because workers described their own moods.
- ru: В абзаце C обе группы стали меньше уставать, но у группы, гулявшей в парке, улучшение было заметнее. Поэтому неверно, что пользу получила только группа из парка. Никаких тестов не проводилось: работники сами описывали своё настроение.
- uk: В абзаці C обидві групи стали менше втомлюватися, але в групи, що гуляла в парку, покращення було помітнішим. Тож неправильно, що користь отримала лише група з парку. Жодних тестів не проводили: працівники самі описували свій настрій.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: A ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**37.** What did Matthias Okeke find about shoppers who arrived on foot?

- A. Their single visits were worth more than those of drivers.

## Тест gt-7

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2278. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Keeping Fit in Brackenford: Five Options Compared

A. Riverside Leisure Centre is run by the town council and is the obvious choice for anyone who wants to swim as well as train. Alongside a 25-metre pool, it has a reasonably equipped gym, a sports hall and changing rooms that were completely rebuilt last year. There is no contract: you can either pay for each visit or buy a monthly pass, which can be cancelled at any time. Residents who bring a council tax bill or another proof of address receive a third off the standard price, so it is worth sorting out your paperwork before you go. The downside is that the centre gets very crowded between five and seven on weekday evenings, when queues form for the most popular machines. If your schedule allows it, mid-morning or the hour before closing is far more pleasant. Swimming lessons for children are popular and tend to fill up within days of being announced.

B. Ironworks Strength Club, tucked behind the railway station, is a very different place. Its owner, Dorothea Pemberton, competed in weightlifting for over a decade, and the club is aimed squarely at people who want to lift heavy weights rather than run on treadmills; there are only two cardio machines in the whole building. Members receive an electronic key that lets them in around the clock, but nobody is given one until they have completed a one-hour induction with a member of staff. Pemberton insists that beginners are welcome and says many of her current members had never touched a barbell before joining. Membership is charged monthly, with a minimum commitment of three months. Apart from a free technique session on Saturday mornings, there are no group classes. The changing facilities are basic, so some members shower at home.

C. If you travel a lot for work, Pulse Fitness may suit you best. It belongs to a national chain, and a standard membership gives access to more than eighty branches across the country, not just the one on the top floor of the Brackenford shopping centre. The gym floor is large and modern, and the price includes around forty classes a week, from cycling to boxing-style workouts. Members can park free for up to two hours in the shopping centre car park. However, joining usually means signing a twelve-month contract, and leaving early is expensive. There is normally a joining fee as well, although this is waived for anyone who signs up in January. Some members complain that the most popular classes are fully booked within minutes of opening for reservations.

D. Willow Studio offers no gym equipment at all; instead, it specialises in yoga and Pilates. Classes are limited to twelve people, so instructors can correct each participant's posture, and the studio is particularly popular with people who sit at a desk all day. It is run by Anselm Wiradja, a former physiotherapist, who designs a gentle beginners' course every term. Places must be booked through the studio's app, and anyone who cancels less than twelve hours before a class is charged in full. Newcomers can try one class without paying, which is a sensible way to find out whether the atmosphere suits you. Rather than a membership, you buy packs of five or ten classes, which remain valid for six months.

E. Finally, the Harbour Road Community Gym is a charity run largely by volunteers. Its equipment is older and the space is small, but it has a warm, unhurried atmosphere that appeals especially to older adults and people returning to exercise after illness or injury. Every Tuesday and Thursday, Nkechi Halvorsen, a retired nurse, leads a supervised session for anyone who feels nervous about training alone. Fees are based on what members can afford: those on a low income or receiving benefits pay a reduced rate, and nobody is asked to prove their earnings. The gym opens only on weekday mornings and two evenings a week, and it closes completely for the first two weeks of August while volunteers carry out repairs. For those willing to work around these hours, it offers remarkable value.

#### matching_information: The passage has five paragraphs, A–E. Which paragraph contains the following information? Write the correct letter, A–E. NB You may use any letter more than once.

- **A** A
- **B** B
- **C** C
- **D** D
- **E** E

**1.** a facility that members can enter at any hour of the day or night

- en: Paragraph B says the Ironworks key lets members in "around the clock", which means at any hour. The Harbour Road gym in E has limited hours, so it does not fit.
- ru: В абзаце B сказано, что ключ Ironworks позволяет членам клуба приходить "around the clock", то есть в любое время суток. Зал Harbour Road из абзаца E работает только в определённые часы, поэтому не подходит.
- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що ключ Ironworks дає змогу членам клубу приходити "around the clock", тобто в будь-який час доби. Зал Harbour Road з абзацу E працює лише в певні години, тому не підходить.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: B ✓.

- uk: В абзаці E сказано, що зал Harbour Road повністю зачиняється на два тижні в серпні. Січень згадано в абзаці C, але лише як місяць, коли не стягують вступний внесок.

- en: Paragraph A says residents with proof of address get a third off. The reduced rate in E depends on income, not on where people live.

**4.** a membership that can also be used in other parts of the country

- uk: В абзаці D сказано, що Willow Studio стягує повну вартість, якщо скасувати заняття менш ніж за дванадцять годин до початку. Дороге дострокове припинення в абзаці C стосується розірвання договору, а не скасування запису.

### Секция 2: Taking On Your First Employee

A. Hiring your first member of staff is one of the biggest steps a small business owner will take. Overnight, you stop being responsible only for yourself and become responsible for someone else's income, safety and working conditions. This guide from the Eastbrook Business Advice Office sets out the main tasks involved and the order in which to tackle them. It is not a substitute for professional legal advice, and because employment rules differ from one country to another, you should always check the details that apply where you trade. Our advisers offer free one-to-one sessions every Tuesday morning for anyone who wants to talk through their plans.

B. Before you write an advertisement, ask yourself whether you genuinely need an employee. If the extra work comes in short bursts, a freelancer paid by the job may be a cheaper and more flexible solution. If you decide that a permanent role makes sense, put together a job description that sets out the main duties, the expected hours and the rate of pay. This document will shape your advertisement, guide your interviews and later form the basis of the contract, so it is worth taking time over it. Then work out what the post will really cost. Wages are only part of the picture: you may also have to pay employer contributions to the state, fund paid holidays and buy equipment or uniforms. Marguerite Oyelowo, who has advised local firms at our office for over a decade, tells clients to add about a fifth to the basic wage when they draw up their budget. 'Owners who plan around the wage alone are often caught out by the extra costs within the first few months,' she says.

C. Once the budget is settled, deal with the official side. In many countries you must register as an employer with the tax authority before the first payday, because you will be responsible for deducting income tax from your employee's wages and passing it on. You will also need a way of running payroll; many small firms use inexpensive software, while others pay an accountant to handle it. Also find out whether the law requires you to hold insurance that covers claims from staff who are injured or fall ill because of their work. In some places this cover is compulsory from the day your first employee starts, and the fines for not having it can be heavy.

D. With the paperwork under way, you can start recruiting. Advertise in places your candidate is likely to look, and ask every applicant the same core questions at interview so that you can compare them fairly. Before making a firm offer, check that your chosen candidate has the legal right to work in the country. This usually means seeing original documents, such as a passport, in the person's presence and keeping a dated copy on file. Photocopies or photographs sent by email are not enough. It is also sensible to contact at least one previous employer for a reference, although a reference should never replace your own judgement.

E. Your new employee is entitled to clear written terms. In many countries these must be provided on or before their first day, not weeks later once they have settled in. As a minimum, the document should state the job title, pay, working hours, holiday entitlement and how much notice either side must give to end the arrangement. If you include a probation period, say how long it lasts and what will happen at the end of it.

F. The first week deserves careful planning. A proper induction should cover health and safety procedures, the location of first-aid equipment, how to deal with customers and, above all, whom to ask when something is unclear. Sanjay Whitlock, who runs a bakery on Mill Street, admits he got this wrong. 'My first assistant was simply handed an apron and told to get on with it,' he recalls. 'She left after three weeks.' Since then he has used a one-page checklist for every new starter, and his second recruit has now been with him for four years.

G. Finally, keep accurate records of the hours your employee works, the pay they receive and any holiday they take, as you may be asked to produce these. Arrange a short review at the end of the probation period, and after that make time for regular conversations rather than waiting for problems to build up. Staff who feel listened to are more likely to stay, and replacing someone usually costs more than keeping them.

### Секция 3: Growing Together

A. On a corner of Harwick Street, where a burnt-out shop once stood, there are now raised beds of beans, a bench made from old scaffolding boards and a hand-painted sign inviting passers-by to come in. Ten years ago the site was fenced off and littered with broken glass; today around forty households share it. Scenes like this have become increasingly common. In towns and cities across many countries, neglected corners of land are being turned into community gardens – spaces where neighbours grow food and flowers side by side rather than behind their own fences. Councils that once regarded such sites as eyesores are, in some cases, now actively offering them to residents' groups. Waiting lists for a plot can run to several years in some places, a sign of how far demand has outstripped supply.

B. It would be wrong, however, to see this as a purely modern trend. During both world wars, governments in Britain and the United States urged citizens to dig up lawns, parks and even sports grounds to grow vegetables, and millions responded. Much later, in the 1970s, residents of some American cities began clearing rubbish from abandoned lots and planting them, often without asking anyone's permission. Some of those early plots still exist today, surrounded by expensive apartment blocks. What has changed is the motivation. Wartime gardeners grew food because they had to; most of today's volunteers could easily buy their carrots at the supermarket, and many admit that their home-grown ones end up costing far more. So why do they bother?

C. Kavita Ellingsen, a social researcher who spent two years visiting gardens in three cities, believes the answer has less to do with vegetables than with company. In her study, which involved interviews with more than 200 members, only a small minority named fresh food as their main reason for joining. Far more mentioned meeting neighbours, escaping loneliness or simply having somewhere to go. 'People told me again and again that they came for the tomatoes and stayed for the conversations,' she says. Ellingsen found that older members in particular valued the garden as a place where friendships formed naturally, without the awkwardness of an organised social event. She is careful to point out, though, that her study looked only at people who had already joined, so it cannot say whether gardening itself makes people less lonely.

D. Keeping a shared garden running smoothly takes more organisation than visitors might expect. Lucia Brannigan, who coordinates the Harwick Street site, explains that every member signs a short agreement before receiving a plot. It sets out simple rules: no chemical weedkillers, no planting of trees that will shade other beds, and a commitment to spend at least two hours a month on shared tasks such as turning the compost or repairing paths. Watering is organised through a rota posted on the door of the tool shed, so that nobody's crops die while their owner is on holiday. Newcomers are usually given a smaller bed for their first season, to see whether they can keep up with it. Members who repeatedly ignore the rules can eventually be asked to give up their plot, though she says this has happened only twice. 'The rules sound strict,' Brannigan admits, 'but they actually prevent arguments. When everyone knows what's expected, there's very little to fall out about.'

E. Not every garden enjoys such a stable existence. Many are created on land that is only borrowed, sometimes from a council and sometimes from a private owner waiting for the right moment to build. When that moment comes, years of work can disappear within weeks. Brannigan's own group came close to losing its site when the owner received an offer from a developer; it survived only after members raised enough money to buy a long lease. Brannigan estimates that the campaign took eighteen months and involved almost every member. Other risks are less dramatic but just as serious. Gardens often depend on a handful of committed volunteers, and when those people move away or grow tired, enthusiasm can quickly fade. There can also be friction between long-standing members, who may resist change, and newcomers with fresh ideas about what the garden should be.

F. For anyone thinking of getting involved, Marcus Tembo, a retired bus driver who has belonged to the Harwick Street garden since it opened, has some straightforward advice. Start small, he says: a single bed tended well is more satisfying than a large plot overgrown with weeds. Ask experienced members questions, since most are delighted to share what they know. And don't expect to save money – seeds, tools and the occasional failed crop add up. 'If you want cheap vegetables, go to the market,' he laughs. 'You come here for something you can't buy.' Most importantly, he adds, turn up regularly, even when there is little to do: the conversations by the compost heap matter as much as the harvest.

#### matching_headings: The reading passage has six paragraphs, A–F. Choose the correct heading for each paragraph from the list of headings below. There are more headings than paragraphs, so you will not use them all.

- **ii** The lessons of wartime food shortages
- **iii** A transformed corner reflects a growing movement
- **iv** Why growing your own is cheaper than shopping
- **v** Social connection as the real attraction
- **vi** A familiar practice driven by new reasons
- **vii** Threats to a garden's survival
- **viii** Measuring the health benefits of gardening
- **ix** Practical tips from a long-serving member

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**38.** What does the writer suggest about the long waiting lists for garden plots?

- A. Interest in plots has grown faster than the number available.
- B. Many applicants give up before a plot finally becomes free.
- C. Councils have been slow to offer unused land to residents.
- D. Most gardens limit their membership to around forty households.

- Ключ: **A (Interest in plots has grown faster than the number available.)**
- Доказательство: «a sign of how far demand has outstripped supply»
- en: Paragraph A says the waiting lists show that demand has outstripped supply, meaning more people want plots than there are plots. The passage says some councils are now offering land, and 'forty households' refers only to Harwick Street.
- ru: В абзаце A сказано, что листы ожидания показывают: спрос превысил предложение, то есть желающих получить участок больше, чем самих участков. В тексте говорится, что некоторые городские советы уже предлагают землю, а «forty households» относится только к Harwick Street.
- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що списки очікування показують: попит перевищив пропозицію, тобто охочих отримати ділянку більше, ніж самих ділянок. У тексті йдеться про те, що деякі міські ради вже пропонують землю, а «forty households» стосується лише Harwick Street.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: A ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**39.** What does Ellingsen point out about her own study?

- A. It found older members were the loneliest of all.
- B. It cannot establish whether gardening reduces loneliness.
- C. It relied mainly on members who had recently left.
- D. It covered more cities than any earlier piece of research.

- Доказательство: «it cannot say whether gardening itself makes people less lonely»
- en: In paragraph C, Ellingsen says her study only included people who had already joined, so it cannot show whether gardening itself makes people less lonely. It studied current members, not people who had left, and it does not compare older members' loneliness or mention earlier research.

Исследование касалось нынешних участников, а не тех, кто ушёл; в нём не сравнивается одиночество пожилых участников и не упоминаются более ранние работы.
- uk: В абзаці C Ellingsen каже, що до її дослідження увійшли лише люди, які вже приєдналися до саду, тому воно не може показати, чи саме садівництво зменшує самотність. Дослідження стосувалося теперішніх учасників, а не тих, хто пішов; у ньому не порівнюється самотність літніх учасників і не згадуються попередні роботи.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: B ✓.

- A. are frequently ignored by the newer members.
- B. were introduced after a serious dispute arose.

## Тест gt-8

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2310. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Welcome to Ashgrove Community Library

A. Borrowing: Anyone who lives, works or studies in the district can join Ashgrove Community Library free of charge. Bring one form of photo identification and something showing your current address, such as a recent utility bill, to the front desk, and your card will be printed while you wait. Adult members may borrow up to twelve items at a time, including books, audiobooks and DVDs. Most items are lent for three weeks, but new releases and DVDs must be returned after seven days because demand for them is high. You can renew an item twice, online or by phone, provided nobody else has reserved it. We no longer charge fines on children's books, although adults who return items late pay a small daily charge. Returns can be made at any time using the book drop beside the main entrance, even when the building is closed.

B. E-books and digital services: Your library card also gives you access to our digital collection, which includes thousands of e-books, e-audiobooks and current magazines. First, download the free LibraryLink app on your phone or tablet and sign in with your card number and four-digit PIN. If you have forgotten your PIN, staff at the front desk can reset it, but for security reasons this cannot be done over the phone. Up to six digital titles can be borrowed at once, and they disappear from your device automatically when the loan period ends, so there is never anything to bring back. Popular titles often have a waiting list; you will receive an email as soon as your turn comes, and you then have two days to accept the loan before it passes to the next person. Anyone who feels unsure about using the app can book a free half-hour session with one of our volunteer 'digital helpers', who are available on Tuesday and Thursday mornings.

C. Study rooms: On the first floor there are four quiet study rooms, each seating up to six people, as well as a single silent room with individual desks. The group rooms are ideal for revision sessions, small meetings or job interviews held by video call, and each is equipped with a large wall screen and a whiteboard. Members may book a room for a maximum of three hours per day, either online or at the front desk, up to a fortnight in advance. Bookings are free for members; non-members may use a room for a fee of £5 an hour. Please arrive on time: if nobody has checked in within fifteen minutes of the start of a booking, the room may be given to someone else. Food is not permitted in any study area, though drinks in bottles with a lid are welcome. The silent room cannot be reserved and is available on a first-come, first-served basis.

D. Children's events: Our children's area is busy all week. Rhyme Time, for babies and toddlers with their parents or carers, runs every Monday and Wednesday at 10.30 a.m. and lasts about half an hour; no booking is needed. Older children aged five to eleven can join the Saturday Story Club, where they listen to stories, play word games and make simple crafts. Because places are limited to twenty, families should reserve a spot in advance. During the summer holidays, children are invited to take part in our reading challenge: participants who finish six books receive a certificate and a medal at a celebration event in September. Tevita Faleolo, who coordinates our children's events, believes the challenge helps young readers keep up their skills during the long break from school, and he hopes more families will join this year. Please note that children under eight must be supervised by an adult at all times while in the library.

E. Opening hours and other information: The library is open from 9 a.m. to 7 p.m. on weekdays and from 10 a.m. to 4 p.m. on Saturdays; it is closed on Sundays and public holidays. Free Wi-Fi is available throughout the building, and printing and photocopying cost 10p per black-and-white page. Branch manager Ottavia Kerimova encourages members to share their ideas: a suggestion box is located next to the lift, and comments can also be left on our website. 'Many of our recent improvements, including longer Saturday hours, came directly from users,' she explains.

#### true_false_not_given: Do the following statements agree with the information given in the leaflet? Write TRUE if the statement agrees with the information, FALSE if the statement contradicts the information, or NOT GIVEN if there is no information on this.

**1.** A person who studies in the district but lives somewhere else is allowed to join the library.

- Ключ: **TRUE**
- Доказательство: «Anyone who lives, works or studies in the district can join»
- en: Paragraph A says that anyone who lives, works or studies in the district can join, so studying there is enough even if you live elsewhere.

- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що записатися може будь-хто, хто живе, працює або навчається в цьому районі. Тож достатньо там навчатися, навіть якщо ви живете деінде.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: T ✓; GPT-5.4: T ✓; Grok: T ✓.

- Доказательство: «your card will be printed while you wait»

- ru: В абзаце A сказано, что карточку печатают прямо при вас. Значит, новые читатели получают её сразу, а не через несколько дней.
- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що картку друкують просто при вас. Отже, нові читачі отримують її одразу, а не через кілька днів.

**3.** DVDs have a shorter loan period than most other items.

- Ключ: **TRUE**
- Доказательство: «Most items are lent for three weeks, but new releases and DVDs must be returned after seven days»
- en: Paragraph A says most items are lent for three weeks, but DVDs must come back after seven days, which is shorter.
- ru: В абзаце A сказано, что большинство материалов выдают на три недели, а DVD нужно вернуть через семь дней, то есть раньше.
- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що більшість матеріалів видають на три тижні, а DVD треба повернути через сім днів, тобто раніше.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: T ✓; GPT-5.4: T ✓; Grok: T ✓.

**4.** The LibraryLink app can be installed on a desktop computer.

- Ключ: **NOT GIVEN**
- Доказательство: — (NOT GIVEN)
- en: Paragraph B only mentions downloading the app on a phone or tablet. It does not say whether it can or cannot be used on a desktop computer.
- ru: В абзаце B говорится только о том, что приложение можно скачать на телефон или планшет. Можно ли пользоваться им на стационарном компьютере, не сказано ни в ту, ни в другую сторону.
- uk: В абзаці B ідеться лише про те, що застосунок можна завантажити на телефон або планшет. Чи можна користуватися ним на стаціонарному комп'ютері, не сказано ні так, ні інакше.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: NG ✓; GPT-5.4: NG ✓; Grok: NG ✓.

**5.** Members who have forgotten their PIN can get it reset by telephoning the library.

- Ключ: **FALSE**
- Доказательство: «for security reasons this cannot be done over the phone»
- en: Paragraph B says staff at the front desk can reset a PIN, but this cannot be done over the phone, which is the opposite of the statement.
- ru: В абзаце B сказано, что сотрудники на стойке обслуживания могут сбросить PIN-код, но по телефону этого сделать нельзя. Это противоположно утверждению.
- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що працівники на стійці обслуговування можуть скинути PIN-код, але телефоном цього зробити не можна. Це протилежне твердженню.

**6.** All of the digital helpers used to be employed as library staff.

- Ключ: **NOT GIVEN**

- ru: В абзаце B сказано, что цифровые помощники — это волонтёры, которые доступны по вторникам и четвергам утром. О том, где они работали раньше, ничего не говорится.
- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що цифрові помічники — це волонтери, доступні у вівторок і четвер зранку. Про те, де вони працювали раніше, нічого не сказано.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: F ✗; GPT-5.4: NG ✓; Grok: NG ✓. Пересмотр: keep — The passage only says the helpers are volunteers now. Being a volunteer today does not rule out having been library staff in the past, so nothing decides the statement. The solver who chose FALSE confused their current role with their past jobs.

- ru: В абзаце C сказано, что те, кто не записан в библиотеку, могут пользоваться комнатой за £5 в час. Бесплатное бронирование есть только у читателей библиотеки, но остальных не исключают.

### Секция 2: First Aid at Work: What Every Employee Should Know

A. This guidance applies to everyone who works at the Harrowfield distribution centre, including temporary workers and visiting contractors. It explains what our first aiders do, what is expected of you if a colleague is injured or suddenly becomes ill, and how accidents must be reported. Do not assume it only concerns trained first aiders: in most incidents, the first person to arrive is an ordinary employee, and their actions in the first few minutes matter.

B. At present, the centre has fourteen qualified first aiders spread across the three shifts, so at least three should be on site at any time. Their names and phone extensions are shown on the green noticeboards beside each staircase and on the staff intranet. Each first aider has completed a two-day course with an approved training provider, and the certificate they receive is valid for three years, after which a refresher course is required. Our health and safety coordinator, Delphine Asante, also runs an informal practice session every six months, because skills such as resuscitation fade if they are not used.

C. First aiders have a limited role. Their job is to keep the casualty as safe and comfortable as possible until professional help arrives or the person recovers; they are not doctors, and they are not expected to diagnose conditions. For this reason, first aiders will not give out tablets of any kind, not even everyday painkillers, since they cannot know whether a colleague has an allergy or is taking other medication. Before approaching anyone who is hurt, a first aider is trained to check the area for danger, such as moving vehicles or spilled chemicals. This is not selfishness: a rescuer who is injured cannot help anybody.

D. If you are the first to find an injured or unwell colleague, stay calm and do not try to move them unless they are in immediate danger. Contact a first aider by dialling 400 from any internal phone, which connects directly to the security desk; the guard on duty will radio the nearest available first aider. Where the situation is serious, for example if the person is unconscious, has difficulty breathing or is bleeding heavily, call the emergency services first and the security desk second. If an ambulance is on its way, ask a colleague to wait at the main gate to guide the crew straight to the right part of the building. Finally, keep onlookers away, as a crowd gets in the helpers' way.

E. First aid kits are kept in every department and can be recognised by a white cross on a green background. Each kit is assigned to a named first aider, who checks its contents once a month and records the date on a card fixed inside the lid. If you take anything from a kit, even a single plaster, please let that person know so it can be replaced. Eye-wash bottles are also mounted in the loading bays. These are sealed for hygiene reasons and must be thrown away once opened or once the expiry date has passed, whichever comes first.

F. Every accident at work must be entered in the accident book, however minor. The book is held at reception, and the entry should be completed within 24 hours, ideally by the injured person. If they are unable to do so, a witness or the first aider who dealt with the incident may write it instead. The record should state the date, time and exact location, describe what happened and the injury caused, and note any treatment given. Stick to facts: the book is not the place for opinions about who was to blame. Once a page has been completed, reception staff remove it and file it in a locked cabinet, because it contains personal information. Ms Asante reviews new entries every Friday and reports serious incidents to the authorities when the law requires it.

G. We also ask you to report near misses, that is, events which could have caused an injury but did not, such as a pallet falling into an empty aisle. These are recorded on a yellow form available from any supervisor. Ravindra Coulibaly, a senior first aider, points out that the most common injury he treats is a cut hand from opening boxes with ordinary craft knives. A review of last year's records, including several near misses with knives, led the company to switch to safety cutters with retractable blades. Nobody will be criticised for making a report; the aim is to learn, not to punish.

### Секция 3: All Aboard the Night Train

A. For much of the twentieth century, falling asleep in one city and waking up in another was an ordinary part of European travel. Sleeper carriages linked capitals across the continent, and for many families an overnight journey was the only affordable way to reach a distant coast or visit relatives abroad. By the early 2000s, however, that world seemed to be fading. Low-cost airlines could carry passengers across the continent in a couple of hours for less than the price of a bunk, while new high-speed lines made many daytime rail journeys short enough to finish before dinner. One by one, overnight routes were cut from the timetables.

B. The trouble was partly financial. Night services are expensive to operate: a sleeping car holds far fewer people than an ordinary carriage, and because the train spends the night on a single long journey, it cannot be used for several shorter trips during the day, as a normal train would be. In 2016 Germany's national rail operator withdrew its own sleeper services, arguing that they had been losing money for years. Many observers assumed that this decision marked the end of the story.

C. It did not. Austria's state railway took over several of the abandoned routes and began investing in new carriages, betting that demand had been underestimated rather than lost. Within a few years the gamble appeared to be paying off, and other operators followed its example. Today, travellers can once again board an evening train in cities such as Vienna, Zurich or Brussels and step off the next morning hundreds of kilometres away. On some lines, demand has been so strong that tickets for summer weekends sell out months in advance. A handful of new routes have also been launched by small private companies, some of which hire their locomotives and carriages instead of buying them, a way of keeping start-up costs low.

D. Part of the explanation lies in changing attitudes to flying. A growing number of travellers, particularly younger ones, say they want to reduce the carbon footprint of their holidays, and rail produces far lower emissions per passenger than air travel over the same distance. Yet according to transport researcher Elspeth Moravec, concern for the planet is only part of the picture. In a survey of more than two thousand passengers on four overnight routes, she found that the reason given most often for choosing the train was a practical one: it saved the cost of a hotel room. "People like to feel virtuous," she says, "but what really persuades them is arriving in the city centre, rested, without having paid for an extra night's accommodation." Environmental motives came second, ahead of comfort and a dislike of airport security queues.

E. Not everyone is convinced the revival will last. Gideon Sarkis, who spent twenty years planning timetables for a large operator, points out that night trains face obstacles that daytime services rarely meet. Track maintenance is usually carried out after dark, so overnight trains are sometimes sent on slower diversions, and each country along the route may charge its own fee for using the rails. "A train crossing three borders can receive three separate bills," he explains, "and in the end it is the passengers who cover the difference." In his view, ticket prices will remain the biggest barrier unless governments agree on common rules for charging trains that cross their borders. He is not against night trains, he stresses; he simply wants their supporters to be realistic.

F. Passengers themselves report mixed experiences. Fares vary enormously, from the price of a reclining seat to several hundred euros for a private compartment with its own shower. Delays are common, and some newer carriages have been criticised for being cramped. Food on board is usually simple, often little more than a bread roll and coffee at breakfast. Even so, many travellers describe the journey as an experience in itself. Corinna Valtonen, a teacher who regularly takes an overnight service to visit her sister in another country, compares it to "a small hotel that happens to move". She admits that she rarely sleeps through the night, but says the hours spent reading or watching the countryside slip past the window are part of the pleasure rather than time wasted.

G. Whether night trains become a mainstream choice again may depend less on nostalgia than on investment. Operators need new rolling stock, which can take years to design and build, and booking a journey that crosses several national networks is still far more complicated than buying a plane ticket. Moravec believes that a single booking website covering every European operator would do more for night trains than any advertising campaign. For now, though, the sight of a sleeper pulling out of a station at dusk is no longer a scene from a museum. It is, once again, simply a way of getting somewhere.

#### multiple_choice_multi: Choose TWO letters, A–E, for each question.

**34–35.** Which TWO reasons does the writer give for why night trains were expensive to run?

- A. Staff working through the night must be paid higher wages.
- B. Each sleeping car has room for relatively few passengers.
- C. Passengers expected breakfast to be included in the fare.
- D. Old sleeping cars needed frequent and costly repairs.
- E. The trains cannot be reused for shorter trips in the daytime.

- Доказательство: «a sleeping car holds far fewer people than an ordinary carriage, and because the train spends the night on a single long journey, it cannot be used for several shorter trips during the day»

## Тест gt-9

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2294. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":6,"high":6}.

### Секция 1: Summer Camps for Local Children

A. Set in a privately owned forest twenty minutes from town, Hollybrook offers week-long residential stays for children aged 11 to 15. Camp leader Lorenzo Kask spent fourteen years as a park ranger before opening the camp, and his knowledge of local wildlife shapes much of the programme. Campers sleep in canvas tents, learn to build shelters and light fires safely, and cook their own evening meal over an open flame. Families should pack a warm sleeping bag, a waterproof jacket and sturdy shoes; a full kit list is sent out after booking. To encourage children to focus on their surroundings, mobile phones are handed in on arrival and given back on the final morning. Parents who want news of their child can ring the camp office between 7 and 8 pm on any evening. A week costs £395, including all meals.

B. Little Makers is a day camp for younger children, aged 5 to 8, held in the main hall of the Elm Street Community Centre. Each week has a different theme, such as 'under the sea' or 'space travel', and children paint, model with clay and build large cardboard sculptures linked to that theme. Doors open at 8.30 am and the day finishes at 4 pm. Staff stay until 4.15, but after that a charge is added for every quarter of an hour, so please plan your journey accordingly. Lunch is not provided: children need a packed lunch and a refillable water bottle every day. Because several regular attendees have serious allergies, the centre is completely nut-free, and staff check lunchboxes each morning. A week costs £140, and when two or more children from the same family attend, each additional child receives 15% off.

C. Run by qualified instructors at Saltmere Harbour, Coastline Sailing Camp introduces children aged 9 to 13 to small-boat sailing over five consecutive days. Mornings are spent on the water in single-handed dinghies, while afternoons cover knots, weather and basic navigation in the clubhouse. Safety comes first: before a place is confirmed, every child must show that they can swim 25 metres unaided, either at the harbour pool or at a local leisure centre, which then signs a form. Life jackets and wetsuits are provided, so children only need a swimming costume, a towel and a hat. Since the harbour is some distance from town, a coach collects campers from the railway station at 8 am and brings them back at 5 pm, at no extra cost to families. Instructor Bernadette Okwu points out that sessions may move indoors if winds become too strong. The camp costs £260 per week.

D. Code and Create is a two-week technology camp for teenagers aged 12 to 16, based in the computer suite of Northgate Library. No previous experience is required. In the first week, participants learn the basics of a beginner-friendly programming language; in the second, they plan, design and test a simple video game of their own, working in pairs to give each other feedback. Laptops are provided, so there is no need to bring one from home. Coordinator Ayesha Thornbury explains that the aim is to show young people that technology is something they can create, not just consume. On the final Friday afternoon, parents, grandparents and friends are invited to a showcase where every participant presents and demonstrates their finished game. Places are limited to twenty, and the fee for the full fortnight is £310. Sessions run from 10 am to 3 pm.

E. Green Thumbs Farm Camp takes place at Mill Lane Farm, a working family farm on the edge of town, and is designed for children aged 7 to 11. Farmer Fenella Draycott and her team show campers how to feed the goats and chickens, collect eggs and look after the vegetable beds. Children plant seeds on their first day, and at the end of the week each child goes home with a basket of the salad leaves and herbs they have helped to grow. Mud is unavoidable, so campers should wear old clothes and bring wellington boots. The camp costs £175 a week, but a limited number of bursaries covering the full fee are available for families on low incomes. Applications for these must reach the farm office by 30 April, and successful families are informed in mid-May.

### Секция 2: Time Off: Annual Leave and Sickness Absence

A. This section explains how holiday entitlement works at Fernleigh Freight Services and what you should do if you are too unwell to work. Full-time employees receive 25 days of paid annual leave each year, in addition to public holidays. Part-time colleagues receive the same entitlement on a proportional basis, and because working patterns vary so widely, their allowance is calculated in hours rather than days. The leave year runs from 1 April to 31 March. Staff who join part-way through the year receive a share of the full allowance that reflects their start date.

B. All requests for annual leave must be submitted through the online staff portal; requests made verbally or by email will not be recorded. As a general rule, you should give at least twice as much notice as the amount of time you wish to take, so a week's holiday should be requested no later than two weeks in advance. Your line manager will normally reply within five working days. Requests for July, August and late December are dealt with strictly in the order in which they arrive. A manager may decline a request if granting it would leave more than a third of the team absent at the same time. For this reason, please do not make any travel bookings until your request has been approved, as the company will not cover costs you have already paid.

C. We encourage everyone to take their full allowance, but we recognise that this is not always possible. Up to five unused days may be carried over into the following leave year, but these must be taken by the end of June; any days still unused after that date will be lost. Exceptions are made only where an employee has been unable to take leave because of long-term illness or parental leave. Untaken holiday cannot be exchanged for extra pay, except when an employee leaves the company, in which case any remaining days are added to the final salary payment.

D. If you are unable to come to work because of illness, you must contact your line manager by telephone as early as possible, and in any case no later than one hour after your usual start time. Text messages and emails are not acceptable, because your manager may not see them in time to arrange cover. When you call, explain briefly what is wrong, how long you expect to be away and whether any urgent tasks need to be passed to a colleague. If your manager cannot be reached, ask to speak to the duty supervisor instead.

E. For absences of up to seven calendar days, including weekends, you do not need to see a doctor. Instead, you should complete a self-certification form on the portal on the day you return. If you are off for longer than this, you must provide a medical certificate from your doctor covering the whole period. After every absence, however short, your manager will hold a brief return-to-work conversation with you. This is an informal chat rather than a formal interview: its aim is to check that you are well enough to be back and to bring you up to date with anything you have missed.

F. Once you have completed your three-month probationary period, you are entitled to company sick pay. This means full pay for up to four weeks of absence in any rolling twelve-month period, followed by half pay for a further four weeks. Employees who are still on probation receive only the minimum sick pay required by law. If you fall ill while on annual leave, the days you are unwell can be treated as sick leave and your holiday returned to you. To do this, you must report the illness on the first day it occurs, even if you are abroad, and later supply a medical certificate.

G. If an employee has four separate periods of sickness within six months, a review meeting will be arranged with their manager and, where appropriate, our occupational health service. The purpose of this meeting is to identify any support that might help, such as adjusted hours or changes to equipment; it is not a disciplinary step. Routine medical and dental appointments should be arranged outside working hours wherever possible. If this cannot be done, you may either make up the time later in the same week or use annual leave. Questions about any part of this policy should be directed to our HR adviser, Gulnara Abenova.

- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: D ✓.

### Секция 3: Behind the Forecast

A. When you glance at the weather app on your phone and see a small cloud with a raindrop beside tomorrow's date, it is easy to forget how much work went into that tiny symbol. Behind it lies one of the largest cooperative scientific efforts on the planet, involving thousands of instruments, some of the most powerful computers ever built and a great many trained specialists. Grete Mapondera, a senior forecaster with twenty years' experience at a national weather service, likes to compare the process to an iceberg. 'The public sees only the tip,' she says. 'Everything that makes it possible is out of sight.'

B. Every forecast begins with measurement. Thousands of weather stations on land record temperature, air pressure, humidity and wind several times an hour. Twice a day, at the same moment all around the world, balloons are released carrying small instrument packages that radio back readings as they rise through the atmosphere. Ships, buoys and commercial aircraft add further data, while satellites watch clouds, measure the temperature of the sea surface and track water vapour from far above. Even so, coverage is uneven. Large areas of ocean and remote land have few instruments, and these gaps are one reason why forecasts for some regions are less dependable than others.

C. Raw measurements, however, cannot simply be fed into a computer. Instruments occasionally fail or give readings that disagree with their neighbours, and observations arrive at different times from different places. Before any prediction can be made, meteorologists must turn this untidy collection into a single, consistent description of the atmosphere at one moment, known as the 'initial state'. This step, called data assimilation, blends the new observations with the previous forecast for the same time, giving more weight to measurements that are considered reliable. Many specialists regard it as the least glamorous but most important part of the whole process, because a forecast can never be better than its starting point.

D. With the starting picture complete, the real calculation begins. Forecasting models divide the atmosphere into a three-dimensional grid of boxes, stacked in layers from the ground to well above the height at which aircraft fly. For each box, the computer applies the laws of physics that govern how air moves, warms, cools and releases moisture, working out what will happen a few minutes ahead, then repeating the step again and again until it reaches several days into the future. The finer the grid, the more detail the model can capture, but each reduction in box size multiplies the number of calculations. This is why major weather services rely on some of the fastest supercomputers in existence. Even so, many features, such as a single thunderstorm, are smaller than one box and have to be estimated rather than calculated directly.

E. There is a deeper difficulty. The atmosphere is what scientists call a chaotic system, meaning that tiny errors in the starting picture grow larger as time passes, until the forecast eventually bears little resemblance to reality. Since no set of observations is perfect, forecasters cannot escape this problem, but they have found a clever way to measure it. Instead of running the model once, they run it perhaps fifty times, each with slightly altered starting conditions. If most versions produce similar weather, confidence is high; if they spread out in different directions, the outlook is genuinely uncertain. This approach, known as ensemble forecasting, is behind many of the percentages you see in forecasts.

F. Given all this computing power, it might seem that people are no longer needed. Mapondera disagrees. Models, she explains, still struggle with local effects: fog that settles in a particular valley, or a sea breeze that keeps a coastal town several degrees cooler than the city inland. Experienced forecasters know these patterns and adjust the computer's output accordingly. They also decide which of several competing models to trust on a given day and, crucially, judge when a situation is serious enough to issue a warning. 'A model can tell you a storm is likely,' she says, 'but someone has to decide how loudly to sound the alarm.'

G. Explaining the forecast to the public may be the hardest step of all. Idris Calder, a researcher who studies how people use weather information, surveyed around 1,500 adults and found that fewer than half correctly understood a '40% chance of rain'. Many believed it meant that rain would fall for 40% of the day, or over 40% of the area. In fact, it means that, on days with similar conditions, rain would be expected at any given spot on about four occasions out of ten. Calder argues that forecasters should put as much effort into clear communication as into accuracy. Accuracy itself, meanwhile, keeps improving: a forecast for several days ahead is now roughly as reliable as a next-day forecast was in the early 1980s.

### Секция 1: Moving House: A Five-Step Checklist

A. If you rent, check your tenancy agreement for the notice period you must give your landlord; in many cases it is a month, but some contracts ask for more. Next, decide whether you will hire a removal firm or move your belongings yourself. Anwar Tasker, who manages a removal company, recommends booking at least four weeks ahead, particularly if you hope to move at the end of a month, when demand is highest. 'Most people want to move on a Friday, so the first slots to disappear are those,' he says. Ask each firm for a written quote and find out whether insurance for breakages is included or costs extra. This is also a good time to sort through cupboards: anything you sell, donate or throw away now is something you will not have to pack, carry or pay to transport.

B. Contact your current energy and water suppliers to tell them the date you are leaving, so that you stop being charged from that day. On the day itself, read every meter at both the old and new property and photograph the figures; a dated image is the simplest way to settle a disagreement about a final bill. Do not assume you must stay with the supplier the previous occupant used. You can usually switch once you have moved in, although it may be worth checking whether any early-exit fee applies to a fixed-term deal at your old address. Broadband deserves special attention, because connecting a new line can take two weeks or longer. Arrange it well in advance if you work from home, and keep your mobile data allowance in mind for the first few days.

C. However carefully you update your address, some letters will still go to your old home. A paid redirection service, offered by the postal service in many countries, forwards your mail to the new address for a chosen period, often three, six or twelve months. Apply at least a week before moving, since the service does not begin immediately. Redirection should be treated as a safety net rather than a solution, however. Write a list of everyone who sends you post — your bank, employer, pension provider, insurers and any magazine subscriptions — and inform each of them directly. Important documents such as bank cards may not be forwarded at all, for security reasons. It is also sensible to leave your new address with a trusted neighbour, who can pass on anything that slips through.

D. Soon after you arrive, contact the local council about any local property tax and, where relevant, waste collection; some areas issue recycling bins only on request. Adding yourself to the electoral register at your new address is a separate step in many places, so do not assume the council will do it automatically. Yolanda Prescott, a housing adviser who has helped hundreds of families relocate, says the step people most often postpone is finding a doctor. 'They wait until a child is ill, and then discover the nearest practice is not taking new patients,' she explains. Her advice is to register as soon as you have the keys, and to look for a dentist at the same time, as waiting lists can be long. Parents should contact the education authority early about school places, which are usually allocated by area.

E. Pack a single box of essentials to travel with you rather than in the van: phone chargers, medicines, a kettle, basic tools, toilet paper and a change of clothes. Before you leave the old property, walk through every room, check cupboards and the loft, and take photographs to show the condition you left it in, which may help if there is a dispute about your deposit. Hand over all keys and get a receipt if possible. In your first week, update the address on your driving licence and vehicle documents; in some countries failing to do so can lead to a fine. Finally, find out where your nearest pharmacy and supermarket are before you need them. Small preparations like these make the first evenings in a new home far less stressful.

### Секция 2: Team Leaders Wanted at Northgate Distribution

A. Northgate Distribution is looking for eight team leaders to join its regional centre in Halbrook. Over the next year the site will open a new chilled section for fresh food, which means more orders, more vehicles and more people on every shift. Successful applicants will each be responsible for a team of between twelve and fifteen warehouse operatives, working either a day pattern (6 a.m. to 2 p.m.) or a night pattern (10 p.m. to 6 a.m.). Both are permanent, full-time positions, and the starting salary is reviewed after the first six months.

B. So what does the job actually involve? According to site manager Owen Kariuki, it is less about giving orders than people imagine. 'A good team leader isn't someone who walks around with a clipboard,' he says. 'They start every shift with a short briefing, share out the work fairly, keep an eye on accuracy and sort out problems before they grow.' When someone calls in sick or a delivery arrives late, the team leader decides how to move people around so that orders still leave on time. Owen adds that the quality he values most is the ability to stay calm when things go wrong. 'I'd much rather have someone who owns up to a mistake straight away than someone who hides it until the end of the shift.'

C. You do not need to have held the title of supervisor before. We ask for at least one year's experience in a warehouse, retail or delivery setting, along with good spoken and written English and basic computer skills. A forklift licence is useful but not essential, since we can arrange the training. Current Northgate employees are encouraged to apply. Dev Sandoval, who joined as a picker four years ago and now leads the night team in the ambient section, says the biggest adjustment was not the paperwork but the relationships. 'Suddenly I was giving feedback to people I'd been having lunch with the week before. You have to be fair to everyone, including your friends.'

D. New team leaders are not left to work things out alone. Training lead Mirjam Tanaka runs a six-week induction that begins with a fortnight of shadowing, during which each newcomer works alongside an experienced leader on the same shift pattern they will later take over. This is followed by a short course on coaching and handling difficult conversations. 'People often assume the system is the hard part,' Mirjam says, 'but most can learn the stock software in a few days. Listening well takes longer.' Night-shift leaders receive an additional allowance, and all staff have access to a pension scheme and a subsidised canteen.

E. Shortlisted candidates will be invited to an assessment day at the Halbrook site. Recruitment coordinator Yara Bellamy explains that it lasts about four hours and has three parts: a guided tour of the warehouse floor, a group exercise and a one-to-one interview. 'Please bring photo ID and proof of your right to work in the UK,' she says. 'For the tour you must wear closed-toe shoes. If you don't own safety boots, tell us your size in advance and we'll lend you a pair.' Candidates who arrive late may not be able to join the tour, because groups leave reception on the hour.

F. In the group exercise, candidates are given a realistic problem, such as planning a shift when three operatives are absent and a large order has just come in. Yara stresses that the assessors are not looking for the loudest voice in the room. 'We notice who listens, who checks that quieter people have had their say, and who keeps an eye on the clock.' Health and safety officer Luka Petrović also sits on the panel. He pays particular attention to whether candidates raise safety without being prompted. 'On the tour, I'm interested in people who spot a blocked fire exit or a pallet stacked too high and mention it. That tells me more than any answer they've rehearsed.'

G. The final interview is usually led by Owen himself. He asks every candidate to describe a real occasion when a plan fell apart and what they did next, so it is worth preparing two or three examples from your own working life. Dev's advice is simple: 'Be honest about what you'd do differently now.' To apply, complete the online form by 28 March. If you need any adjustments for the assessment day, contact Yara directly before you attend.

#### summary_completion: Complete the summary below. Choose NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS from the passage for each answer.

### Секция 3: The Long Road to Your Teacup

A. Tea is now drunk in almost every country, yet few drinkers give much thought to how it is made. Whether it arrives as a delicate pale green brew in a Japanese bowl or as a strong, milky mug in a British kitchen, almost every cup of tea in the world begins in the same place: the leaves of a single evergreen shrub, Camellia sinensis. Herbal infusions such as peppermint or chamomile are often called tea, but strictly speaking they are something else. Black, green, oolong and white teas are not made from different plants, as many drinkers assume. What separates them is what happens to the leaves in the hours and days after they are picked, and those changes begin long before any kettle is switched on.

B. Left alone, a tea plant would grow into a small tree several metres tall. On plantations, however, the bushes are pruned to roughly waist height, creating a flat, dense surface that growers call the plucking table. In the growing season, new shoots appear on this surface every week or two, and these are what the pickers are after. The traditional rule is 'two leaves and a bud': only the tender tip of each shoot is taken. Sabitha Moorcroft, who manages a hillside tea garden, says that experienced pluckers can gather many kilograms a day without slowing down. 'Machines are quicker and cheaper,' she admits, 'but they can't tell a fresh shoot from a tough old leaf, so they take both. For our better teas, nothing replaces a trained pair of hands.'

C. For most teas, once the baskets reach the factory, the leaves are spread out in long troughs, and warm air is blown through them for many hours. This stage, known as withering, removes a good deal of their moisture and turns the leaves soft and floppy, rather like spinach left out of the fridge. Soft leaves can then be rolled without snapping. Rolling, done by hand on small estates and by machine on large ones, twists the leaves and breaks open their cells, so that the juices inside come into contact with the air. It looks like a simple mechanical step, but it sets off the chemistry that gives each tea its character.

D. That chemistry is oxidation, a process often wrongly called fermentation. Enzymes in the bruised leaf react with oxygen, and the leaves gradually darken, much as a cut apple turns brown on a kitchen counter. Allowed to continue for an hour or more, the reaction produces the coppery colour and full, malty flavour of black tea. Oolong makers stop the process partway, which is why oolongs range from almost green to nearly black. Green tea is heated soon after picking, by steaming or pan-firing, so the enzymes are destroyed before they can do their work and the leaves keep their colour and fresh, grassy taste. White tea is handled least of all: it is simply withered and dried. Whatever the style, the leaves finish with a final drying, which brings their moisture so low that they will keep for months.

E. Before tea reaches the shops, it passes through the hands, and mouths, of professional tasters. Haruto Ebdon spends his mornings in a tasting room where dozens of white bowls stand in a row, each holding a sample from a different estate. He slurps every spoonful noisily, a habit that startles visitors but sprays the tea across the palate so that no flavour is missed. His main job is blending. A single garden's tea changes with the weather and the season, yet customers expect their favourite brand to taste the same every time they buy it. Blending is sometimes suspected of being a way to hide cheap leaf, but Ebdon insists that consistency is the real aim: 'I might combine twenty teas from four countries just so that this year's packet tastes exactly like last year's.' Long before this, at the factory, the leaves will have been sorted by size: whole leaves are sold loose, while the smallest broken pieces, which release their flavour quickly, mostly end up in tea bags.

F. The final stage of the journey takes place in our own kitchens, and it is the one we most often get wrong. Black tea generally suits water straight off the boil, but more delicate teas do not, and green and white teas in particular can turn bitter if the water is too hot. In one study, the food scientist Clémence Adjei asked volunteers to compare cups of the same green tea brewed at different temperatures without telling them which was which. Most preferred the cup made with water at around 80°C, and many described the boiling-water version as harsh and bitter. Adjei admits her study was small, involving only twenty-four volunteers, but her advice is easy to follow: let the kettle stand for a few minutes before pouring. After so many skilled hands have shaped the leaf, it seems a pity to ruin it in the last thirty seconds.

#### matching_headings: The passage has six paragraphs, A–F. Choose the correct heading for each paragraph from the list of headings below. Write the correct number, i–ix. There are more headings than paragraphs, so you will not use them all.

- **i** One plant, many teas
- **ii** A careful harvest of the newest growth
- **iii** Softening and breaking the leaf
- **iv** How exposure to air shapes colour and flavour
- **v** Experts who keep a familiar taste the same
- **vi** The final step happens in the kitchen
- **vii** Why machines are replacing human pickers
- **viii** The health benefits of drinking green tea
- **ix** How tea spread from China to the rest of the world

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**38.** According to Sabitha Moorcroft, the main drawback of harvesting tea by machine is that it

- A. gathers older leaves as well as new shoots.
- B. damages the flat surface of the bushes.
- C. is slower than an experienced plucker.
- D. costs more than employing trained pickers.

- Ключ: **A (gathers older leaves as well as new shoots.)**
- Доказательство: «they can't tell a fresh shoot from a tough old leaf, so they take both»
- en: In paragraph B, Moorcroft says machines cannot tell fresh shoots from old leaves and take both. She says machines are quicker and cheaper, so the options about cost and speed are wrong, and she never mentions damage to the bushes.
- ru: В абзаце B Moorcroft говорит, что машины не могут отличить свежие побеги от старых листьев и срывают и то и другое. По её словам, машины быстрее и дешевле, поэтому варианты о стоимости и скорости неверны, а о повреждении кустов она вообще не упоминает.
- uk: В абзаці B Moorcroft каже, що машини не можуть відрізнити свіжі пагони від старого листя і зривають і те, і інше. За її словами, машини швидші й дешевші, тому варіанти про вартість і швидкість неправильні, а про пошкодження кущів вона взагалі не згадує.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: A ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**39.** Why are green tea leaves heated soon after they are picked?

- A. to reduce their moisture for long storage
- B. to prevent their reaction with oxygen
- C. to make them soft enough to be rolled
- D. to give them a coppery colour and flavour

- Ключ: **B (to prevent their reaction with oxygen)**
- Доказательство: «so the enzymes are destroyed before they can do their work and the leaves keep their colour»
- en: Paragraph D says heating destroys the enzymes before oxidation, the reaction with oxygen, can happen. Softening comes from withering, the final drying for storage applies to all teas, and the coppery colour belongs to black tea.
- ru: В абзаце D сказано, что нагревание разрушает ферменты до того, как может начаться окисление — реакция с кислородом. Размягчение происходит при завяливании, окончательная сушка для хранения применяется ко всем чаям, а медный цвет характерен для чёрного чая.
- uk: В абзаці D сказано, що нагрівання руйнує ферменти ще до того, як може початися окиснення — реакція з киснем. Розм'якшення відбувається під час в'ялення, остаточне сушіння для зберігання стосується всіх чаїв, а мідний колір властивий чорному чаю.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: B ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**40.** What does Haruto Ebdon say is the purpose of blending tea?

- A. to include teas from as many countries as possible
- B. to disguise poorer leaf from some estates
- C. to keep a product's flavour unchanged over time
- D. to produce a new taste for each season

- Ключ: **C (to keep a product's flavour unchanged over time)**
- Доказательство: «just so that this year's packet tastes exactly like last year's»
- en: In paragraph E, Ebdon says consistency is the real aim, so that this year's tea tastes like last year's. Hiding cheap leaf is only what some people suspect, and he mentions four countries as an example, not as a goal.

- uk: В абзаці E Ebdon каже, що справжня мета — сталість: щоб цьогорічний чай смакував так само, як торішній. Приховування дешевого листя — лише те, що дехто підозрює, а чотири країни він згадує як приклад, а не як мету.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: C ✓; GPT-5.4: C ✓; Grok: C ✓.
